    (FUN_0045a6d0) or through a virtual call into a "spawn from package" fallback.  Returns
    0 when the index is out of range, which means a unit with no actor table can never
    produce an actor from this path.

  So actors come from the UNIT resource's own table, and every structural unit's table is
  empty (9H.16.1).  Physics for crypt geometry therefore does NOT travel this path either.
  The actor-definition fields the engine names in its own strings (CollisionTriangles,
  CollisionPlanes, CollisionConvexMasks, CollisionSpheres, SelfCollision,
  SelfCollisionIndices) do not appear as thin hashes in any of the 691 units, so the actor
  table is a compiled struct, not a key/value store.

STILL UNKNOWN, NOW NARROWED
    Which of the remaining header fields is read by whoever creates the collision for a
    placed unit, and what it points at.  The candidates are the fields no decompiled
    function has yet been seen reading: u32[3], u32[4], u32[5]/u32[7], u32[8]/u32[9],
    u32[12]/u32[13], u32[15]/u32[16], u32[18]/u32[19], u32[20]/u32[21], u32[22], and
    u32[24] (the per-unit 8-byte array).  The way to find it: decompile the READERS of
    those fields - i.e. whoever consumes the level resource besides the loader - rather
    than reading the file for the nth time.

================================================================================
9H.20 THE COLLIDER IS THE DONOR'S 8x8 PATCH, AND IT DOES NOT SCALE
================================================================================

  MEASURED IN GAME, by mapping the collider instead of looking at the plane.  ActorProbe
  casts a 5x5 grid of downward, floor-filtered raycasts over +/-10 around the unit and
  records the surface height at each point.  Our plane (a 40 m quad, entity scale 10, so
  400 m of VISIBLE geometry) gave:

        -      -      -      -      -          rows are y = -10, -5, 0, +5, +10
        -      -      -      -      -          cols are x = -10, -5, 0, +5, +10
        -      -  +0.02  +0.02      -
        -      -  +0.02  +0.02      -
        -      -      -      -      -

  So the collider spans roughly x,y in [0, +8) - an 8x8 patch anchored at the UNIT'S OWN
  ORIGIN, which is exactly the donor mesh's extent (blocks/d01_crypt/floors/
  cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c is 9 verts spanning 0..8, all at z=0).

  *** CORRECTION, from the next run.  The line here used to say "and it does not scale with
  the entity".  That was an over-read of the scale-10 measurement above.  Scaling the tile to
  0.2 collapsed the footprint to a SINGLE point - roughly 1.6 units, i.e. 8 * 0.2 - so the
  collider DOES follow the entity scale, and 625 tiles at 8 spacing with 1.6-unit patches left
  gaps, which made the player fall through MORE, not less.  What the two measurements together
  say: the collider tracks the entity scale downward, and at scale 1 and scale 10 it reads
  ~8 units.  The safe rule is the one the real floor follows - author the block at its own 8
  units, corner-origin, and place it at scale 1. ***

  WHAT THAT MEANS FOR THE PLAYER, and it matches the report exactly ("near the middle I
  DON'T fall through, but moving outwards I do"): they spawn on the unit's origin, which is
  a CORNER of the collider, and can walk about 8 units before stepping off it.  The 400 m
  plane they are looking at has almost no collision under it.

  WHY A REAL FLOOR WORKS: it is TILED.  The same probe on a real floor block
  (unit 423, one of the shell's own) shows the identical signature - one actor, one shape,
  type=3, a hit 0.02 above its own origin - and its raycast hits a DIFFERENT unit id, i.e.
  its neighbour, because the crypt lays hundreds of 8x8 blocks side by side and the patches
  tile into a continuous floor.  A single block is 8x8 of collision too; there are just
  hundreds of them.

  CONSEQUENCE FOR plane_mod: one plane instance can never cover the room.  Either
    1. TILE it - many instances of our unit, each carrying its own 8x8 patch, which is
       exactly how the game builds floors; the visible mesh must then be corner-origin
       (matching the patch) and small (scale ~0.2 so the 40 m quad becomes 8), or
    2. replace the trailer's collision data with a bigger shape - the collider is carried
       in the trailer, our splice copies it verbatim, and its layout is not yet decoded
       (the one thing 9H.19's "the physics is in the trailer" turned out to mean in
       practice: a baked copy of the donor's collision geometry, not a parametric shape).
  Note that templating the SAME unit family differently does not help - the patch comes
  from the template, not from the level or the entity transform.

  *** RESOLVED, AND IT WORKS.  Option 1.  plane_tile.unit is now the donor's equal - 1437
  bytes, 9 verts, x 0..8, y 0..8, z 0, corner-origin, placed at scale 1 - and
  make_room_levels.py lays 625 of them at 8-unit spacing (25x25, reaching +/-96 around the
  spawn).  Confirmed in game: the player spawns on it and walks across it.  Confirmed by
  measurement: the footprint around a tile went from a SINGLE POINT to a filled 3x3 block,
  because the tile's own 8x8 patch now has neighbours filling the rest -

        before (40 m quad, scale 0.2)        after (8 m corner-origin tile, scale 1, tiled)
        -      -      -      -      -        -      -      -      -      -
        -      -      -      -      -        -      -      -      -      -
        -      -  +0.02      -      -        -      -  +0.02  +0.02  +0.02
        -      -      -      -      -        -      -  +0.02  +0.02  +0.02
        -      -      -      -      -        -      -  +0.02  +0.02  +0.02

  So a floor of our own, in a room of our own, in the campaign, with the player spawning on
  it - the thing this whole thread was for. ***

  DEPLOY NOTE, because it wasted a test round: the game's own mods/bundles/<Name>/ copy is
  what the loader re-places into contents/<hash>, and a stale bundle there is served with no
  error at all.  bundle_control.py reported STATE MATCHES REQUEST while the game still had the
  previous build (md5 43910d7a vs 47a1ba93), so two rounds of geometry feedback were about
  files that were never deployed.  Verify three md5s: dev, game mods/bundles, game contents.

9H.17.4 THE OBJECT-SET ACTIVATION PASS - the one place a unit becomes real

    load_level -> FUN_00428a50 -> FUN_0042b6b0 (build Level)
                                  -> FUN_0042f3a0 (0,0) at the tail
                                     -> FUN_0042c620  *** per-unit activation ***

  FUN_0042c620 walks two arrays on the unit and creates each item only when its
  OBJECT-SET id matches, destroying the ones that do not:

      count at [unit + 0x14c]   MESHES,  created with FUN_0045db50(mesh, object_set, 0xff)
      count at [unit + 0x18c]   ACTORS,  created with FUN_0045a890(actor, 0)
                                          destroyed with FUN_0045ba60(actor)
      actor array at [unit + 0x194]; each actor's object-set id is at [actor_def + 8]

  This is what "object sets" means (World.load_level_with_object_sets, and
  Level.set_data(level, "object_sets", ...) in world_proxy.lua) and it is the pass that
  turns a placed unit into a live thing.  It creates meshes and actors - and nothing else.

  The mesh path does not build collision either: FUN_0045db50 fetches the mesh and calls
  FUN_0043fbf0 only when [world + 0x420] is non-zero, and FUN_0043fbf0 turns out to be a
  lookup in a 0x14-byte-stride table keyed on (mesh, object_set) - a per-object-set
  variant/override table, not a physics call.  So a placed unit's mesh is brought in for
  RENDERING and its actor (if its resource has one) for PHYSICS, and a unit with neither an
  actor nor a matching object set gets nothing.

  RULED OUT so far, cumulatively:
    1. actors inside the unit resource   - the whole block family has an empty actor table
    2. flow in the level                 - no create_actor / a_collision / physics strings
    3. the level loader                  - its callees contain no physics factory
    4. the object-set activation pass    - meshes and actors only, no collider built
    5. a level 'physics' resource        - no such name in the 147,168-entry database
    6. a packet-format physics blob      - the level files contain none of the packet magics
                                           (TAG0 / SDKV / DATA / TYPES / TBODY / THSH ...)
    7. a baked triangle-mesh collider    - no long run of coordinate-like floats in any
                                           section of either level

  Which leaves the scene/physics commit path - static geometry extracted for the physics
  world rather than attached to a unit - as the remaining candidate, and the physics module
  is where to look next.  The cheap way to settle it first, without touching the binary:
  raycast down onto the crypt floor IN GAME and log Actor.unit(hit_actor)'s type.  That
  names the collider directly, and it decides whether the plane wants a block template
  (9H.16.2) or something else entirely.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
9H.17.5 WHAT A UNIT SAYS ABOUT ITSELF: nav_grid_type_auto
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  (See also 9H.19: the game's own build script generates this field, and because
  "no physics actors -> ignore" is tested FIRST, a value of blocker/floor/slope proves the
  unit has physics actors.  That is what turned this from a curiosity into the answer.)

  A unit's trailer carries a data value `nav_grid_type_auto`, and it is the cleanest
  classification of a unit the data has.  Read out of all 691 extracted .unit files:

      value      meaning in the game                     examples
      -------    -------------------------------------   --------------------------------
      blocker    solid - blocks the AI                   crypt_wall_4c, cryptsmooth_wall_4c/2c,
                                                         pillars, railings, crypt_rock_01,
                                                         crypt_brick_02, big_sarcophagus,
                                                         blackbox_2c_2c, plank_thick, spawners
      floor      walkable surface                       cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c,
                                                         caverock_floor_4c_4c, brazier_floor_02
      slope      stairs                                 crypt_stairs_low_4c
      ignore     not part of navigation                 borders, corner pieces, skulldoors,
                                                         rockpiles, stalagmites, cobwebs,
                                                         mushroom trees, banners, torches,
                                                         chains, cloth, fog_volume
      (absent)   nothing of its own                     characters, equipment, effects, decals,
                                                         lights, most gameobjects (gold, food,
                                                         carryables, potions, keys)

  The vocabulary is `lua/dungeon/nav_grid.lua`'s - `Unit.get_data(unit, "nav_grid_type_auto")
  or "ignore"`, and nav_grid returns "blocker" / "ignore" as its own type names, so this is
  AI NAVIGATION, not the player's physics.  That is exactly the trap: `blocker` correlates
  with solidity (walls yes, cobwebs no) but it is consumed by Lua for pathfinding, and it is
  NOT what makes the engine collide with a wall.

      BUT IT IS THE EVIDENCE.  9H.19 found the build script that WRITES this field, and it
      tests "no physics actors -> ignore" before every other rule - so a value of
      blocker/floor/slope is a fingerprint that the unit HAS physics actors.  The field does
      not cause collision; it records whether the unit has anything to collide with.  Used
      that way it settles the crypt: walls and floors have physics, cobwebs and torches do
      not.

  A second, stronger correlation exists in the level file itself: the 24 units that carry
  `a_collision` are 24 of the 24 gameobjects that MOVE, and the `blocker` units carry none
  (9H.16.1).  Two independent signals, neither of which is the collider.

================================================================================
9H.18 A REAL BUG: THE ENTITY ROTATION WAS READ WITH z AND w SWAPPED
================================================================================

  Found while cross-checking 9H.6 against the data, and it matters because it silently
  corrupted every level we AUTHORED.

  THE BUG.  resource_tools/level_tools.py read the record as

      rot_z = data[0]    rot_x = data[60]    rot_y = data[64]    rot_w = data[68]
      "rotation": [rot_x, rot_y, rot_z, rot_w]

  i.e. it took z from offset 0 and w from offset 68.  The engine-verified layout is the
  vector part at 60/64/68 with w reconstructed (9H.6, level_to_glb.py both say so; the
  module contradicted its own sibling).  So `rotation` was really (x, y, <not a rotation
  component>, z) wearing the label (x, y, z, w).

  WHY NOTHING CAUGHT IT.  decode -> encode is self-consistent, so the module kept its
  byte-exact guarantee: level_tools.py selftest still PASSES 255/255 on the corrected code.
  The damage is only in AUTHORING: level_set_entities.py writes the rotation through this
  encoder, so every entity built for plane_mod went in with its w in the z slot and its z
  in offset 0.  A spec of [0,0,0,-1] - identity - therefore wrote a 180-degree Z rotation
  (harmless for the flat plane, whose normal is Z, which is why it looked fine, and it is
  the origin of the "(0,0,0,-1) identical to (0,0,0,1)" note in 9H.15).

  THE MEASUREMENT.  Counts how many of a level's cryptsmooth_wall_4c records decode to one
  of the four orientations the ENGINE actually uses for that template (Data/runtime_units/
  modules_d01_crypt_room_crypt_floor_01_adventure.json, 338 instances):

      engine: (0,0,1,0) x99   (0,0,0.70711,-0.70711) x89
              (0,0,-0.70711,-0.70711) x77   (0,0,0,-1) x73

      329 cryptsmooth_wall_4c records of the crypt shell:
          (x,y,z,w) = (f60,f64,f68, -sqrt(1-x*x-y*y-z*z))     100.0%   <- correct
          w read from offset 0 instead (x,y,z,f0)              63.5%
          level_tools.py as it was (x,y,f0,z)                  63.5%

  Sign-insensitive on purpose: q and -q are the same rotation, and both reconstruction
  branches therefore score 100%.  Offset 0 correlates with w (it is -1.0 on identity walls,
  -0.7071 on 90-degree ones, 0.0 on 180-degree ones) but is NOT reliably w - reading it as w
  reproduces only 63.5% - so it stays a raw pass-through field named `flag`.

  THE FIX (applied).  level_tools.py now reads the vector part at 60/64/68, reconstructs
  w as the negative branch, keeps offset 0 verbatim as `flag` for a byte-exact re-encode,
  and on a NEW entity writes the negative-branch w to offset 0 (which is what every
  measured wall record holds).  A spec of [0,0,0,-1] now writes offsets 60/64/68 = 0 and
  offset 0 = -1.0, which is exactly the identity wall.  Verified: selftest still 255/255
  byte-exact, and the tool's own decode now scores 329/329 = 100.0% against the engine.

  CONSEQUENCE FOR plane_mod.  The level files already built (our_level.level,
  room_retarget.level, ...) carry the old, wrong rotation.  Nothing needs re-running for the
  plane - a Z-axis rotation of a Z-normal quad is invisible - but any future level with a
  rotated entity should be regenerated with make_room_levels.py now that the encoder is
  right.

================================================================================
9H.19 THE COLLISION QUESTION, ANSWERED  *** 9H.16.1 WAS WRONG ***
================================================================================

  A unit collides if and only if it has PHYSICS ACTORS, and the game says so itself - in a
  build script that ships in the source tree:

      foundation/scripts/generate_nav_grid_script_data.lua

          local physics_path = file:gsub(".unit", ".physics")
          if common.exists(physics_path) then
              physics_actors = common.sjson_from_file(physics_path).actors
          else
              physics_actors = {}
          end
          local collides_with_anything = #physics_actors > 0
          local nav_grid_type_auto = get_nav_grid_type(path, collides_with_anything)

  So: each unit may have a SIBLING `.physics` SJSON file, its actors are the colliders, and
  `#physics_actors > 0` is the definition of "collides with anything".  That file is not
  shipped separately - it is compiled INTO the .unit - which is exactly what 9I found when
  the `physics` type turned up in no bundle.

  THE SCRIPT ALSO WROTE THE FIELD I DECODED.  It generates the unit's data section:

      sjson.data.walkable, sjson.data.unwalkable, sjson.data.shadow_meshes,
      sjson.data.nav_grid_type_auto, sjson.data.nav_grid_type_override

  which is precisely the data table read out of the compiled units in 9H.16.1 (the
  walkable/unwalkable mesh arrays and `nav_grid_type_auto = "blocker" | "floor" | ...`).
  So `nav_grid_type_auto` is generated build metadata - and because
  `if not collides_with_anything then return "ignore"` comes BEFORE every name rule,
  the value is a FINGERPRINT of whether the unit has physics:

      "blocker" | "floor" | "slope"   =>  the unit HAS physics actors  =>  it is a collider
      "ignore"                        =>  either no physics, OR the name rule fired first
                                          (sandpile, carry, border, debug_whitecube, ...)

  Applied to the crypt shell (the 9H.17.5 census): cryptsmooth_wall_4c/2c, the pillars and
  the railings are "blocker", the floors are "floor", the stairs are "slope" - so THE
  CRYPT'S WALLS AND FLOORS DO HAVE PHYSICS ACTORS - while caves_cobweb_01, fog_volume,
  torch_01 and the banners are "ignore", i.e. no physics, i.e. walk-through decoration.
  That is exactly how the room behaves in game.

9H.19.1 THE CORRECTION: `a_collision` IS A FLOW PARAMETER

  What made 9H.16.1 conclude "structural units have an empty actor table" was a string search
  for `a_collision`, and it was reading the wrong thing.  Walk a unit's sections (FINDINGS
  9.2) and print what follows the materials section, and the strings are:

      ham_plate         'gameobjects/food/ham_plate' 'FlowCallbacks' 'apply_velocity'
                        'linear_velocity' 'actor' 'angular_velocity_random' 'unit' ...
      crypt floor       'floor'          and nothing else
      invisible wall    'blocker'        and nothing else

  `a_collision` sits in that flow data - it is the NAME of an actor that a flow node creates
  (FlowCallbacks.create_actor -> Unit.create_actor(unit, name), 9H.17.3), which is why only
  the gameobjects that MOVE mention it.  The physics actors store no name strings here, so a
  string search could never have found them.

  Nothing else in 9H.16.2 was wrong: actors in the level, flow in the level, the loader, the
  activation pass, a standalone physics resource, packet blobs and baked float triangles are
  all still ruled out, each on its own evidence.  The mistake was ruling out the ONE path
  that was left, on the strength of a string that belonged to flow.

9H.19.2 WHY THE BINARY ALREADY FITTED

  With the mechanism known, the functions found in 9H.17 are the whole story and nothing is
  missing:

      the unit resource carries an actor table   count at [unit + 0xbc], array at [unit + 0xc4]
      FUN_0045a890 instantiates one actor        index -> the actor, or 0 if out of range
      FUN_0042c620 the activation pass           per unit: meshes (+0x14c) and actors (+0x18c),
                                                 each created only when its object-set id
                                                 matches - and `param_3 == 0`, i.e. a level
                                                 loaded WITHOUT object sets, creates them all
                                                 (World.load_level vs
                                                  load_level_with_object_sets)

  So a placed unit's collider is created by the object-set activation pass, out of the unit's
  own compiled physics, for every level that does not ask for object-set filtering.  No
  engine-side "level geometry collision" concept is needed, which is why hunting for one kept
  failing.

9H.19.3 THE FIX, AND IT IS ALREADY BUILT

  plane_mod/plane_block.unit is the 40 m quad spliced into
  `blocks/d01_crypt/floors/cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c` - a unit whose nav type is "floor", so by
  9H.19 it has physics actors - and glb_to_template_unit.py carries the template's trailer
  over VERBATIM.  Measured:

      template  cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c   1437 B    mesh 767    trailer 670
      plane_block.unit                   1104 B    mesh 434    trailer 670
      trailer carried over byte-identically: True   md5 c4146b949aa8381f020f469460f085c5

      plane.unit (deployed now, ham)     2568 B    mesh 434    trailer 2134
      its trailer strings: FlowCallbacks, apply_velocity, linear_velocity,
                           angular_velocity_random

  The deployed plane therefore carries ham_plate's FLOW (velocity and random spin) and
  nothing else; plane_block.unit carries the floor block's physics instead.  That is the whole
  difference between a plane that tumbles and a plane that stands still - and it is why
  9H.16.2's "swap the template" was right for the wrong reason.

  Two things to expect from the test, stated in advance so the result is informative either
  way: the plane should be STATIC, and it should be walkable.  If it is static but not solid,
  the trailer is not being read the way this says and the compiled-physics layout is the next
  thing to decode (it is byte-packed, and it starts, in every unit, with a small header whose
  second word is a count - 2 for the floor block and the invisible wall, 4 for crypt_brick_02,
  5 for ham_plate).
================================================================================
9H.22 THE LEVEL'S AXIS IS TWO QUESTIONS, AND THE TOOL ANSWERED ONE  *** Oct 2026 ***
================================================================================

  WHAT WAS WRONG.  level_from_glb.py asked "is this GLB Y-up?" once - by looking at the axis
  the floor tiles share - and then used that single answer for the positions AND the
  rotations.  A Blender export can answer the two halves differently, and the export that
  built every custom level so far did:

      layout (node POSITIONS)   flat on Z   - an engine-space arrangement
      meshes (unit GEOMETRY)    thin on Y   - the engine's own mesh turned on its side

  The engine applies the record's rotation to ITS OWN unit mesh (engine Z-up), so a record
  built from that file needs one extra composition that the single flag never applied.  Every
  placed unit went in rotated 90 degrees about X: floors standing up, walls lying down.

  THE TWO EXPORTS, MEASURED (the same layout, one corrected)

    Base.glb            (trashed; built crypt_arena01/02/03_custom, hub_custom,
                         custom_duckomp_room)
        layout   flat on Z
        meshes   124 floor meshes, averaged vertex NORMAL (0, -1, 0)   ->  -Y
        rotations  q_old = q_new * R_x(-90)   for all 204 nodes present in both files
    test_arena_03.glb   (the corrected export)
        layout   flat on Z
        meshes   48 floor meshes, averaged vertex NORMAL (0, 0, 1)     ->  +Z
        rotations  yaw about Z - the engine's own convention for structural blocks

  The corrected export is engine-space in every respect, and that was checked against the
  .blend it came from rather than assumed: Blender's +Y-up export applies R_x(-90) to the
  whole scene, so all 208 objects match `R_x(-90) * (Blender scene)` - positions, rotations
  and mesh extents.  q_new is therefore the ground truth the old file's rotations were
  compared against, and 204 of 204 agree.

  THE RULE THE TOOL NOW USES.  With F the frame change between the file's world and the
  engine's, and F' the one between the file's MESH frame and the engine's:

      R_record = F^-1 * R_node * F'

  F is measured from the WORLD up (the floor normal R_node * mesh_up), F' from the MESH up
  (the averaged normal of the flat meshes' vertices).  Both are rotations about X by a
  multiple of 90 degrees, both are read out of the file's own data - the normals are the one
  measurement that separates the two exports above - and for a self-consistent export F == F'
  so the rule is the conjugation it should be.  Positions use F^-1 alone.

  WHAT SHIPPED WRONG (non-yaw records; the bodies' own counts in brackets)

      crypt_arena01_custom.level   1029 of 1570   (the body has 94)
      hub_custom.level              446 of  468   (the body has 6)
      custom_duckomp_room.level    1400 of 4247   (the body has 450)

  They are rebuilt with the corrected tool; see 9H.22.1 for the current build.

9H.22.1 THE CRASH, AND WHAT THE LEVEL IT CRASHED ON LOOKS LIKE

  exception 0xC0000005 at 00488344, access read at 0xEB64DF50
    #0 00488344  FUN_00488340, which is `return *param_1`
    #1 00428ad8  inside FUN_00428a50, the World.load_level delegate (the call at 0x428ad3)
    #2 0059c1f1  the Lua VM running the dungeon generator

  FUN_00488340 is a getter, so the fault is its ARGUMENT.  The call site is inside
  FUN_0042f3a0 - the per-level activation pass - at 0x4300fe:

      MOV EDX,[ECX+0x80]        ; ECX = the level object being activated
      LEA EAX,[EDX+ECX]         ; a SELF-RELATIVE offset field
      CALL 0x00488340           ; read [EAX]  <- faults

  and the branch is guarded by `[placement_table + 0x38] != 0`, i.e. by the level having a
  SECOND placement record: this is the SUB-LEVEL path.  (The earlier crash in the same
  function, 0x430224, is the byte store into FUN_0042f120's NULL result - the material-lookup
  failure documented in 9H.21.)

  The level it crashed on, custom_duckomp_room.level, was built on the crypt SHELL's body
  (room_crypt_floor_01_adventure_template, 4247 units) and shipped at
  modules/d01_hub_room/custom_duckomp_room, i.e. loaded as a HUB room.  So it carried

      metadata  'modules/d01_crypt/shading_environment'   (the hub's own level says
                'modules/d01_hub_room/shading_environment')
      sub-levels  modules/d01_crypt/prefabs/gate_exit/prefab_crypt_gate_exit_02 and
                  modules/d01_crypt/prefabs/floor_details/prefab_crypt_floor_detail_pit_round_01
      spawns  none - a shell has no player_spawn_marker, so the tool had nothing to align our
              floor to and printed "no marker has one of our floors within 8 units"

  The hub's own level (hub_pristine.level, 468 units, the body of hub_custom) has 0
  placements and 4 player_spawn_markers.  Rebuilt on THAT body the level keeps the hub's own
  shading environment, has no sub-level for the activation pass to walk, and aligns our floor
  to the hub's spawn markers (-2.68 here).  That is the build shipped for the test.

  STILL OPEN: whether the sub-level path is what took the process down.  The fixed build has
  to be run; if it still faults at 0x430106, the field is [obj+0x80] of a sub-level object
  and the next step is to find what writes it (FUN_0042b6b0 builds those objects).





9H.22.2 THE TEST: IT LOADS  *** Oct 2026 - first custom level that does ***
================================================================================

  WHAT WAS SHIPPED AND RUN.  custom_duckomp_room.level built from test_arena_03.glb (the
  corrected export) on the HUB's own body (hub_pristine.level, 468 units): 204 placements,
  20 logic entities kept bit-for-bit, 242 decorations moved aside, the floor aligned to the
  hub's spawn markers (-2.68), 0 bytes changed outside the entity table.

  THE RESULT (modloader.log 05:59, crash_trace.log 05:58)

    * NO CRASH.  crash_trace.log carries only the benign LightFX first-chance exception
      (0xE06D7363).  The 0xC0000005 at 0x488344 that killed the previous build is gone, and
      the run went on into the level's flow.
    * the level came up: `level from World.load_level: 463 unit(s)`, and ActorProbe's floor
      probe found a REAL static collider on unit 146 - a box with half_extents
      (4.025, 4.025, 0.025), a floor-filtered raycast hitting it at z=2.14 - so the placed
      units have physics and the player has something to stand on.
    * placement and rotation: "most things were in the right place and very few rotation
      misalignments" (the author's reading of the run).  That is the two-frame fix doing its
      job; what is left are the 6 genuinely tilted props in the export itself.
    * THE PROBLEM: the level also carried the hub's own flow - the CustomRun guard logged
      `flow callback adjust_light_intensity skipped: unit has no light 'omni'` - and the room
      read as "a bit messy".  A donor body brings the donor's flow, volumes and markers with
      it, and moving the donor's ENTITIES out of the way does not touch any of that.

  So the next step is a level with no donor at all: our entity table, our metadata, and
  nothing else - see 9H.23.

9H.22.3 THE RE-EXPORT, AND WHAT IT BREAKS

  test_arena_03.glb was re-exported (2.5 MB, 206 nodes): the author rotated the scene,
  applied scale/rotation/location, and reports it is still Y-up.  The file now has NO node
  transforms at all - every node is `{"name": ..., "mesh": N}`, each object's transform baked
  into its mesh vertices, and each node carrying its own copy of the mesh (206 nodes, 206
  meshes, 0 materials).

  What that does to the tool, and why it cannot be left silent:

    * POSITION: `node['translation']` is absent for every node, so a build from this file
      places all 203 units at the origin.  The frame report shows it ("ours x 0..0 y -0..-0")
      but nothing refuses.
    * ROTATION: baked into the vertices.  There is no quaternion left to convert, and the
      engine needs one rotation per entity - it rotates ITS OWN unit mesh by the record's
      rotation, so the baked copy in the file cannot stand in for it.

  Both halves are recoverable against a reference that still holds the canonical meshes: the
  world bounding box of the baked mesh gives the position once the canonical mesh's own box
  is known, and a vertex-to-vertex fit gives the rotation exactly when the two meshes are the
  same mesh rigidly moved (the correspondence is by index).  Not from bounding boxes alone -
  a box is symmetric, so its yaw comes back only up to the box's own symmetry.


================================================================================
9H.23 A LEVEL THAT IS ONLY OURS  *** Oct 2026 ***
================================================================================

  WHY.  Every custom level so far was a DONOR's body with our entities patched into its slots,
  so it arrived carrying the donor's flow, volumes and markers: the hub's light-fade callbacks
  ran on our level (9H.22.2) and the room read as "a bit messy".  A level of our own has our
  entity table, our metadata, and nothing else.

  THE SKELETON, AND WHAT IS CONSTANT IN IT.  The smallest level in the corpus is 1,136 bytes
  with 6 units and no logic at all
  (assets/exports-samples/samples/level_24abcdf4b1fb4d4a.level).  Compared field by field
  against the hub (468 units), the arena (1,570) and the shell (4,247), everything that does
  NOT scale with the unit count is a constant:

      24 bytes after the unit table   four 1.0 floats, then 8 zero bytes   same in all four
      the u32[8] section              24 zero bytes                        zero in all four
      the per-unit array              8 bytes per unit, every entry {0, its own offset}
                                                                           same in all four
      the 12-byte-record array        count u32[11]                        0 in the sample
      the {offset, 253} pair          points at the render block           rewritten per build
      u32[29], the placement count    0                                    no sub-levels

  Everything else that moves is an offset, so a level of any size is the same sections in the
  same order with the offsets recomputed.  u32[29] = 0 is the field that matters for the crash
  in 9H.22.1: no placement table means the activation pass never enters the sub-level branch
  that faulted.

  ENTITY IDS.  The shipped ids are random 64-bit values, unique within the file and not
  sequential (checked on the sample, the hub and the arena), so any unique value will do.  The
  tool derives them from the unit and its index, so a rebuild reproduces the same file.

  WHAT IT WRITES.  `level_from_glb.py --scratch` writes
    * one record per placement, with the material taken from the shipped CORPUS - the material
      the game's own levels pair with that kind most often (9H.21) - never from a donor;
    * our own player_spawn_marker, on our floor: a donor body supplies one, a level of our own
      has to place its own or the player starts in the air;
    * the skeleton's skydome, the one thing in a level that is neither ours nor the
      encounter's;
  and nothing else - no volumes, no markers, no leftover scenery, no encounter logic.

  VERIFIED WITHOUT THE ENGINE: level_decode.py's invariants pass, and decode -> encode
  round-trips BYTE-EXACT - the same check level_tools passes on 255 shipped levels, and the
  strongest structural statement available offline.

9H.23.1 RECOVERING A BAKED EXPORT

  The re-export (9H.22.3) has no node transforms at all, so the tool now
    * REFUSES by default and says what to do - re-export without applying the transforms,
      which is the path everything else is verified on - because reading such a file as TRS
      writes every unit at the origin;
    * with `--reference <a level export .glb>`, recovers (position, rotation) from the
      geometry itself:
        EXACT  the baked mesh has the same vertex count as one of the reference's parts, so
               the two are the same mesh rigidly moved and a Kabsch fit is exact - the
               correspondence is by vertex index, which is what makes it exact;
        BOX    the counts differ, so the bounding box decides: the canonical box under each
               axis-aligned orientation, against the baked box.  A box is symmetric, so its
               yaw comes back only up to that symmetry - as much as the engine's own mesh can
               tell apart.

  Measured on the author's file against crypt_arena01.glb (2.9 MB): 201 of 206 nodes recovered
  - 153 exact, 48 from boxes (coffin x19, crypt_wall_4c x13, crypt_wall_convex x6,
  coffin_broken x4, brazier_floor_02 x2, crypt_wall_2c x2), 5 unknown kind, 0 unmatched.  The
  5 unknown-kind nodes are DROPPED and named, never written at the origin - which is the whole
  point of the check.

  MESH PARTS ARE NOT UNITS.  An exported unit arrives as one node per mesh part (a coffin is a
  body and a lid, a brazier is two pieces) while the engine's unit already contains all of
  them.  Placing every part put two complete units on one spot, and with baked geometry the two
  fits disagreed and the pair faced opposite ways.  Parts of one unit sit within a fraction of
  a unit of each other, so they are merged into one entity, preferring a part recovered
  exactly: 31 nodes merged on the author's file, 206 nodes -> 170 entities.

  SHIPPED: modules/d01_hub_room/custom_duckomp_room, 15,080 bytes, 172 records = 170 placements
  + our spawn marker + the skydome, no donor content, no sub-level placements (the first
  version of this file crashed on load - see 9H.23.2 for why, and what fixed it).  The metadata
  is the skeleton's own ('core/rendering/default_outdoor'), because that string and the render
  block that follows it are a matched pair - `--shading` can name another environment, and then
  only the string is ours while the render block stays the skeleton's.


9H.23.2 THE FIRST SCRATCH LEVEL CRASHED, AND WHY

  exception 0xC0000005 at 005fea5a, read at 2be60000 - a page boundary
    #0 005fea5a   inside FUN_005fea30, which is MEMCPY/MEMOVE (the alignment-switch copy
                  loop Ghidra decompiles with its switch table)
  and the stack carried return addresses inside FUN_00430440, which FUN_0042f3a0 calls as
  FUN_00430440(0, unit_count - 1, ...) - once per unit, during the load.

  FUN_00430440's per-unit loop, disassembled:

      MOV ECX,[EBX+8]            ; ECX = the header
      MOV EAX,[ECX+0xc]          ; u32[3]
      ADD EAX,EDX                ; + this unit's index
      MOV EAX,[EAX+ECX]          ; value = *(header + u32[3] + index)
      TEST EAX,EAX / JZ skip     ; 0 means "no data for this unit"
      LEA ESI,[EAX+ECX]          ; the data is at header + value (self-relative)
      CALL memmove(dst, ESI, 4)

  So the section u32[3] names STARTS WITH A 4-BYTES-PER-UNIT POINTER TABLE: one entry per
  unit, each a self-relative pointer to that unit's data, zero for none.  That is why the
  sample's section is 24 bytes - 6 units x 4.

  THE BUG.  The scratch writer copied that section from the 6-unit skeleton and never resized
  it.  At 172 units the loader walked 172 entries, ran off the end of the 24 bytes into the
  metadata and the render block, took THOSE bytes as pointers, and copied 4 bytes from a
  garbage address.  A 1,136-byte sample cannot expose this: it is exactly as long as its own
  unit count needs, so the copy stays inside the file.

  THE FIX.  The section is now 4 x N zero bytes and everything after it shifts.  Verified by
  construction rather than by inspection: writing the skeleton's OWN six entities back through
  the writer reproduces the 1,136-byte file BYTE-FOR-BYTE (entity ids included), and the 4 x 6
  = 24 bytes it generates are exactly the section the sample ships.  The same test also caught
  a second, smaller bug - u32[30] was being written as the {offset,253} pair's offset instead
  of the per-unit array's, one byte wrong in that reproduction.

  LESSON FOR THE NEXT SECTION: every section that is "a constant" in the sample is only a
  constant because the sample has six units.  The two that scale are the entity table (72N)
  and this pointer table (4N) - plus the per-unit array (8N) - and the byte-exact reproduction
  of a shipped level is the test that tells the three apart.


9H.23.3 THE SECOND SCRATCH CRASH, AND THE FIELD NO SHIPPED LEVEL EVER SETS

  exception 0xC0000005 at 00430224 - access WRITE at 00000000
    #0 00430224   MOV byte ptr [ESI + ECX*1 - 1], AL
    #1 00428ad8   inside FUN_00428a50 (World.load_level)
    #2 0059c1f1   the Lua VM running the dungeon generator

  The block that faults, in FUN_0042f3a0:

      MOV EDX,[ECX+0x80]     ; ECX = the level object; +0x80 is the unit table's file offset,
                             ; 128 in every level
      ... guard ...
      LEA EAX,[EDX+ECX]      ; = level + 128 = THE FIRST ENTITY RECORD
      CALL the getter        ; EAX = *(level+128) = the first record's OFFSET-0 FIELD
      MOV [ESP+0xc],EAX      ; ...read as a COUNT
      ... allocate max(count,4) bytes, then store one byte per item ...

  So the engine reads the four bytes at the unit table's own start - the first record's
  offset-0 field, the rotation flag - AS A COUNT, allocates that many bytes and writes one
  byte per item.  A flag of -0.7071 is 0xBF3504EF = 3,207,922,927: the allocation fails, the
  pointer stays NULL, and the byte store faults.

  MEASURED, and this is the whole case: of the 255 .level files in the export, ALL 255 have 0
  in that field.  And on the builds whose outcome is known:

      sample (shipped)         first offset-0 =  0.0000   loads
      hub_pristine (shipped)   first offset-0 =  0.0000   loads
      hub-body build           first offset-0 =  0.0000   LOADED (9H.22.2)
      scratch build            first offset-0 = -0.7071   CRASHED (this section)
      crypt_arena01_custom     first offset-0 = -0.7071   CRASHED (the 0x430224 dump from the
                                                          earlier session)
      crypt template (shipped) first offset-0 =  0.0000   -

  THE FIX, in both writers.  The scratch writer sets records[0]['flag'] = 0.0.  The donor path
  sets ents[0]['flag'] = 0.0 UNCONDITIONALLY - and the unconditional part is the lesson: a
  record the tool builds carries no 'flag' key at all, so the encoder derives the negative
  branch (-|w|) for it, and a conditional guard reading `.get('flag', 0.0)` sees 0.0 and does
  nothing.  That is exactly what happened on the first attempt: the arena levels came out with
  -0.7071 in slot 0 again and the tool reported no change.

  WHAT IT MEANS FOR THE EARLIER CRASH: the write-to-NULL at 0x430224 that an earlier session

9H.23.4 THE FIRST BUILD THAT LOOKED RIGHT, AND THE TWO RECOVERY GAPS IT SHOWED

  THE RESULT, in the author's words: the level loads and "for the most part it's working
  great" - a level with no donor content, its own spawn marker, units where they were authored.
  Out of place: the bottom stair, a few of the walls, and a few of the railings.

  Those are exactly the recovery's two weak spots, and both are now closed.

  1. A KIND WITH NO REFERENCE MESH CANNOT BE RECOVERED.  Five nodes came back "unknown kind"
     and were dropped - crypt_railing_end, crypt_rock_01 (x2), coffin_open and one more -
     because crypt_arena01.glb simply does not contain those units.  --reference is now
     repeatable and merges several exports, and with crypt_arena01 + 02 + 03 +
     room_crypt_floor_01:

         recovered 206 of 206 mesh nodes, 158 exact, 48 from boxes, 0 unknown, 0 unmatched
         176 placed (was 170), 22 kinds (was 19), level 15,584 bytes (was 15,080)

  2. THE BOUNDING BOX CANNOT PICK A YAW.  A wall is symmetric along its length and through its
     thickness, so four of the twenty-four candidate orientations fit its extents equally well
     and the first one won - which is why every wall faced the same way.  The box now only
     SHORTLISTS (candidates within 5% of the best extent fit, at most 12) and the vertex cloud
     decides: the orientation whose vertices land closest to the baked ones.  Measured per kind
     on the author's file:

         crypt_wall_4c       2 -> 4 distinct orientations, and 12 -> 13 records (one had been
                             merged away)
         crypt_wall_convex   2 -> 4
         crypt_wall_2c       1 -> 2
         crypt_rock_01       (newly recovered) 3 distinct

     The EXACT route is untouched - it was already right - and a box-recovered node's position
     now comes from the vertex centroid rather than the box centre.


9H.23.5 THE QUATERNION BRANCH, AGAIN - AND IT WAS THE RECOVERED PATH THIS TIME

  The author's report, node by node, was the perfect test case:

      cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c#289   moved to the right one tile
      crypt_railing_straight_4c#60  rotated maybe 70-80 degrees on the height axis
      coffin_open#957               rotated by 90 degrees on the height axis
      crypt_wall_4c#245, 246, 248   mirrored, and out of place a bit

  THE BUG.  A record stores only xyz and the engine derives w = -|w| (9H.6/9H.18), so q and -q
  are NOT interchangeable in a record: a quaternion with w > 0 must have its xyz negated, or the
  engine builds the MIRROR of it.  to_engine_rotation has done that since 9H.18 - but the
  RECOVERY path (9H.23.1) writes _mat_to_quat's result straight into the record, and a Kabsch or
  candidate fit returns whichever sign falls out.  Measured, with the encoder and the engine's
  own reconstruction:

      recovered q = (0, 0, -0.7071,  0.7071)  ->  engine sees (0, 0, -0.7071, -0.7071)  MIRROR
      recovered q = (0, 0,  0.7071,  0.7071)  ->  engine sees (0, 0,  0.7071, -0.7071)  MIRROR
      recovered q = (0, 0,  1.0,     0.0)     ->  engine sees (0, 0,  1.0,     0.0)     ok (w = 0)
      recovered q = (0, 0,  0.0,    -1.0)     ->  engine sees (0, 0,  0.0,    -1.0)     ok

  A mirror of a yaw is a yaw the other way, which is a wall facing backwards and a coffin 90
  degrees out - exactly what was reported.  Any rotation whose w is 0 is unaffected, which is
  why some units were fine and others were not.

  THE FIX.  _record_branch(q) negates all four components when w > 0, and the recovered path
  uses it.  The geometry is unchanged (q and -q are the same rotation); what changes is what the
  ENGINE derives from the record.

  VERIFIED END TO END, not by inspection: for every one of the 206 recovered nodes, the level's
  own record was read back and its effective rotation - the xyz it stores with the engine's
  w = -|w| - compared against the fitted one:

9H.23.6 THE STATE AFTER THE BRANCH FIX, AND WHAT IT NARROWS TO

  THE AUTHOR'S READING OF THE TWO BUILDS, which is the whole dataset:

      no branch rule   (9H.23.4)  walls MIRRORED, coffin 90 deg off, railing 70-80 deg off,
                                  floor tile moved one tile
      with the rule    (9H.23.5)  the three walls FIXED, and "the others are rotated the same
                                  in the other direction now"

  So the rule is right for the walls and wrong for the coffins and their neighbours.  That is a
  clean split along the RECOVERY ROUTE, which is the only systematic difference between them:

      walls                    BOX-recovered  (the author's mesh is a proxy, not the game's)
      coffins, borders, stairs EXACT-recovered (the author's mesh IS the game's, vertex for
                                               vertex)

  and it is not a data difference in the level: the two builds differ in 82 of 178 records, all
  of them exactly the records the rule touches, and `--no-branch` reproduces the earlier build
  BYTE-FOR-BYTE (md5 2083a28f...), so the A/B the author has already run IS the A/B.

  WHAT THE DATA SAYS, for the record: every one of the 206 recovered nodes' records was read
  back and its effective rotation - the stored xyz with the engine's w = -|w| - matches the
  fitted rotation (187 matched, 0 mismatches), and placing the canonical mesh with it reproduces
  the baked geometry to the millimetre (0.000 mean vertex error for the railing, the coffin and
  the floor tile; 0.02 for the box-recovered walls).  The canonical meshes were also checked
  against the units' OWN exports (view/glb/named/...), not just the level exports: coffin_open

9H.23.7 THE ROTATION RULER - the experiment that ends the guessing

  THE AUTHOR'S THIRD REPORT: crypt_stairs_low_4c#170, crypt_railing_straight_4c#60 and
  crypt_border_straight_4c#57 are "off by about 70-80 degrees", and coffin_open#957 is "still
  off by 90 degrees".

  WHAT THE LEVEL HOLDS for those four, read back out of the shipped file:

      crypt_stairs_low_4c#170        record (0, 0, -1, -0)      yaw 180   off-axis 0.0
      crypt_railing_straight_4c#60   record (0, 0, -1, -0)      yaw 180   off-axis 0.0
      crypt_border_straight_4c#57    record (0, 0, -1, -0)      yaw 180   off-axis 0.0
      coffin_open#957                record (0, 0, -0.707, -0.707)  yaw 90   off-axis 0.0

  Every one is a PURE yaw at a multiple of 90, and every kind's rotations in the level are
  rotations the shipped corpus itself uses for that kind.  A 70-80 degree angle cannot come out
  of a record like that: whatever produces it is either the unit's own mesh frame or the engine
  composing something the export does not show.  Off-axis rotation is 0.0 for all four, so it is
  not a tilt either.

  So: STOP READING RECORDS, RUN THE EXPERIMENT.  `--rotation-ruler` adds four copies of each
  reported kind at 0/90/180/270 degrees - one ROW per kind, one COLUMN per yaw, 10 units apart,
  anchored in the layout's own -x/-y corner so it stands on the floor and can be walked to -
  and ships it with the level.  Reading the four columns of one row says which yaw the engine
  draws the way it should; the difference between that and what was written is the offset, and
  it is the same offset for every kind if the cause is the unit frame and different per kind if
  it is not.

  (The ruler also caught the branch bug a second time on the way in: its own quaternions were
  built in place and went into records unbranched, so the 90 and 270 columns came out swapped.
  write_scratch's record builder now runs EVERY quaternion through _record_branch, whatever it
  came from - a rule that has to hold at every door into the format, not just the one it was

9H.23.8 THE LEVEL UNIT LOGGER, TURNED ON - AND WHAT THE EXISTING CAPTURES ALREADY SHOWED

  The author asked for the level unit logger, which is the right instrument: it records the
  ENGINE's own per-unit transforms, so it settles what the engine does with a record instead of
  what a decoder thinks it does.  It was OFF in the deployed config (`[LevelMining] Enabled=0`,
  AutoWrap=0, AutoSweep=0); it is now Enabled=1 / AutoWrap=1 / AutoSweep=1 in both the game's
  copy and the repo's, so loading a level is enough and one .json per level lands in
  binaries/levels/ next to runtime_units.tsv.

  BEFORE ASKING FOR A RUN, the captures already in Data/runtime_units/ answer a lot.  Joining
  the engine's units to the records of the level they came from, by position with the level's
  own world offset (the arena sits at (4,4,0), which is why a raw position join failed and an
  index join looked plausible for 680 records and then drifted - the engine omits some kinds
  from its unit list entirely, player_spawn_marker among them):

      arena records joined: 1557 of 1570
      STRUCTURAL BLOCKS: floors, walls, borders, railings, stairs, pillars, braziers,
                         encounter doors, spawn fields - 100% of them match the record
      SCATTER PROPS:     skeleton_var01..06 9-38 of 143 mismatch, coffin 7 of 44,
                         decal 6 of 8, chest 3 of 7, skulls, boxes, urns, corpses

  So the record's rotation IS what the engine uses for a structural block - the
  (x,y,z,-sqrt(1-|v|^2)) reading holds there, 100% over 1557 units, which is a far stronger
  statement than the wall-only check in 9H.6 - and for the scatter props the engine does
  something else: it re-rolls them.  That is the engine's own behaviour on its own levels (the
  corpus carries 180 distinct coffin rotations, and the shipped arena shows the same 10-27%
  disagreement), not a property of our file.

  CONSEQUENCE FOR THE AUTHOR'S REPORTS: a "scattered" prop is not something a level file
  controls, and a railing, border or stair IS - the engine will place those exactly as written.
  The next run captures OUR level, and the rotation ruler (9H.23.7) puts four known yaws of each
  reported kind in one row, so the engine's answer for each of them is a direct read.

  found at.)

  373 verts, identical bbox and orientation; crypt_railing_straight_4c 1354 (the unit's 2708 is
  two coincident parts); crypt_wall_4c 565 (the unit's 1130, likewise).

  So the transform cannot be shown wrong from here, and the two things left that it could be
  are (a) the engine's own unit carrying geometry or an internal transform the exports do not
  show, or (b) the author's baseline for "right" differing from the file.  The cheapest way to
  tell them apart is a RIGHT/WRONG PAIR OF THE SAME KIND - the trick that pinned 9H.23.5 in one
  round, when "crypt_wall_4c#245 is mirrored" and the three names beside it pointed straight at
  the branch.  One name that looks right and one that looks wrong, of one kind, is a diff.


      187 records matched (the rest are the merged mesh parts)   rotation MISMATCHES: 0

  and per node, placing the canonical mesh with the record's effective rotation reproduces the
  baked geometry to the millimetre:

      cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c#289    mean vertex error 0.000   placed bbox == baked bbox
      crypt_railing_straight_4c#60   mean vertex error 0.000
      coffin_open#957                mean vertex error 0.000
      crypt_wall_4c#245/246/248      bbox-extent error 0.000, placed bbox within 0.02 of baked
                                     (box-recovered: the mesh is not the game's own)

  So three of the four reported nodes are now exact and the three walls are within 2 cm.  If the
  floor tile or the railing STILL look wrong in game, note that their records were already
  geometrically exact before this fix (both had w = 0): the next thing to look at would be the
  engine's unit for that kind carrying geometry the reference export does not show, not the
  transform.

  SHIPPED: 15,584 bytes, 178 records, 176 placements, 22 kinds, first record's offset-0 field 0.

  STILL OPEN: the bottom stair.  Its data looks right: all six crypt_stairs_low_4c nodes carry
  the same mesh (102 vertices, extents 8 x 2.56 x 1 in engine space), come through the EXACT
  route, and land with one shared orientation and positions stepping cleanly along -y and +z -
  which is what the source has.  So what "out of place" means for it is the next question:
  rotated, moved, or missing.  That separates a recovery error from a level-format one, and it
  is the difference between more recovery work and none.

  recorded as "a zeroed material" is this field, not a material.  Every override build that
  put one of our units in slot 0 hit it; the hub-body build survived only because the hub's own
  slot 0 is a logic entity, and logic entities have 0 there.

9H.24 THE CAPTURE OF OUR OWN LEVEL, AND THE ONE THING IT FOUND  *** Oct 2026 ***
================================================================================

  The level unit logger (9H.23.8) was turned on and the hub-room build loaded.  It wrote
  binaries/levels/modules_d01_hub_room_custom_duckomp_room.json - 192 units of OUR level, with
  the engine's own position and quaternion for each.  scripts/compare_level_capture.py joins it
  to our 194 records; the join is (rounded world position, template hash) TOGETHER, because
  with 24 distinct kinds the hash alone pairs the wrong instances, and 192 of 194 join uniquely.

  FIRST: THE CAPTURE IS FAITHFUL.  The capture's rot array is (x, y, z, w) - reading it that way
  reproduces our record's rotation for the majority of units, and the other order scores 31/446
  against 236/446 on the shipped arena.  The pipeline (LevelProbe -> Mods.Level.put ->
  WriteLevelJson) stores pos and rot from the same unit under the same index, so there is no
  pos/rot mixing.  Positions agree to 0.01 for 192 of 194 records: THE LAYOUT IS RIGHT.

  SECOND: THE ENGINE DOES NOT ALWAYS USE THE RECORD'S ROTATION.

      hub-room build (ours, --scratch)          192 joined    153 exact    39 different  (20%)
      edited crypt room (ours, donor body)      185 joined    177 exact     8 different (4.3%)

  and the differing deltas are not arbitrary - they come in mirrored pairs:

      +90 x9   -70.5 x8   +19.5 x6   +70.5 x5   -90 x4   -19.5 x3   (plus 4 singletons)

  19.47 = arcsin(1/3) and 70.53 = arccos(1/3) are the angles of a rotation pushed through a
  tilted frame, not of a record read straight out.  Every IDENTITY record survives: all 75
  units whose record is 0 degrees come back exactly as written, whatever their kind.

  THIRD: IT IS NOT A FUNCTION OF THE RECORD.  Two units of the same kind whose records are
  byte-identical (checked: the same w and z bit patterns, bf3504ef / 3f3504f7) come back one
  exact and one at 250.5 degrees.  Whatever decides this lives OUTSIDE the entity record.  The
  record is not the problem: it is a well-formed unit quaternion, the layout is the shipped
  layout field for field (w at 0, scale at 4, entity id at 24, template hash at 32, material at
  40, position at 48, rotation xyz at 60/64/68), the metadata is the same four keys, and the
  material hashes are the shipped corpus's own (the cache is built from the game's export tree,
  not from our output).

  FOURTH, AND THIS IS THE FINDING: THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN THE TWO BUILDS IS THE SECTIONS.

  The two rows above are the same kinds, the same recovery, the same writer.  The only
  difference is the level body they were written into: the crypt-room build carries a shipped
  room's sections, the --scratch build carries the 6-unit sample's.  Measured on the files:

      section                  sample (our skeleton)   crypt room (donor body)
      u32[10]/u32[11]          0 records               42 records of 12 bytes
      u32[15]/u32[16]          0 records               present
      u32[18]/u32[19]          0 records               3 records
      u32[29]/u32[30]          placement table absent  7 placements, 56 bytes each

  So the engine re-derives the transform of a fifth of our units when the level has no room
  sections, and almost none of them when it does.  A 12-byte record is three floats - a cell,
  a run, a size - and the crypt rooms are assembled from blocks, so the reading that fits is
  that the engine places the BLOCK kinds (walls, railings, borders, stairs, pillars, floors)
  from the room's own definition, and that the shipped levels' records are the RESULT of that
  placement rather than an input to it.  That is why a shipped level agrees with its capture
  unit for unit, and why our level does not: we wrote a rotation the engine was about to
  compute for itself.

  WHAT THIS MEANS FOR THE TWO OPEN REPORTS
    * the reported kinds (crypt_stairs_low_4c, crypt_railing_straight_4c,
      crypt_border_straight_4c, coffin_open) are exactly the worst hit - 5 of 15, 8 of 16,
      5 of 12 and 3 of 5 units respectively - and the rotation ruler's three non-zero columns
      are all in the differing set.  The engine replaces any non-identity rotation for them.
    * the "scattered" units are the same effect on the prop kinds (coffin 5 of 19, brazier,
      rock, light), which the engine also re-places.

  NEXT, IN ORDER
    1. the author's visual read of the rotation ruler (which of the 0/90/180/270 columns of
       each row looks right) - it says whether what is SEEN is the engine's derived value
       (then the sections are the fix) or our written one (then the capture is measuring
       something else and the level is already right);
    2. a second capture of the same level, to tell a deterministic rule from a re-roll;
    3. carry the donor's room sections in --scratch (u32[10]/[11], [15]/[16], [18]/[19]; not
       the placement table, which is what crashed the activation pass in 9H.22.1) and rebuild.
       If the 20% drops to the crypt-room build's 4.3%, the sections are the whole story.



9H.24.1 THE TWO ANSWERS, AND WHAT THEY CHANGE

  DETERMINISM.  The same level was loaded twice, with the capture copied between runs.  All 192
  units come back with the SAME rotation both times (192 of 192 identical, 0 different), and the
  same 39 differ from the record.  So the engine's value is a FIXED RULE applied to the level,
  not a per-load re-roll: the "scattered props" reading of 9H.23.8 does not survive this.

  THE RULER.  The author's read: "the row on the left side of the room looks like it is rotated
  fine" - and the ruler is four copies of each kind at 0/90/180/270 degrees, one row per kind,
  isolated, 10 units apart.  The capture says three of its four columns are NOT what was written
  (the 0-degree column comes back exact; 90, 180 and 270 come back at 180, 250.5 and 289.5).

  THOSE TWO FACTS TOGETHER MEAN THE CAPTURE IS NOT THE RENDERED TRANSFORM - at least not by
  itself.  If the engine really drew the values the capture reports, three quarters of every
  ruler row would be visibly wrong, and the author says they are not.  So Unit.local_rotation
  (unit, 0) is reading a transform that is NOT the one the unit is drawn with for those units -
  the same caution 9H.6 hit from the other side, where w = f0 managed 65% of a wall's
  orientations and the reconstruction managed 100%.

  WHAT THAT DOES TO 9H.24's CONCLUSION: the 20%-vs-4.3% difference between the two builds is
  still real and still points at the room sections, but it can no longer be called "the engine
  replaces our rotations and that is what the author sees".  The honest state is:

    * positions: right, measured (192 of 194 join to 0.01) - the layout work is done
    * rotations: the capture disagrees with the record for 20% of units, deterministically,
      and the engine's own values are what it stores - but the ruler says those values are not
      what gets drawn, so the capture alone cannot settle the author's report
    * the one instrument that CAN settle it is a level whose content the author can look at and
      name: the reported kinds, isolated, at known yaws (done - the ruler - and it looks right),
      and the same kinds in a RUN, touching, at known yaws (not done yet).  The current room has
      those kinds in runs; the ruler has them isolated; that is the difference between the two
      reports, and it is the next thing to test.

  NEXT: a test level with (a) the reported kinds isolated at 0/90/180/270, (b) the same kinds
  end to end in a run of four at 0 and at 90 degrees, (c) one non-90 yaw (45) of the same kind,
  and (d) two units copied from the level with their RECOVERED rotations, in a clear area.  That
  separates value from context in one look, which is what the author offered to build and what
  the tooling can also generate.

9H.27 THE PROXIES: WHY THE TEST ARENA SCATTERED  *** Oct 2026 ***
================================================================================

  The author's own diagnosis, and it is right: "every piece named was assigned the full unit,
  instead of being our own custom geometry... I do want to be able to use native game props,
  but I think the naming definitely has something to do with it."

  MEASURED, per kind, the node's own extents (sorted) against the game unit's extents that the
  node's NAME resolves to, over the 858 mesh nodes of test_arena_03.glb:

      kind                        nodes  are the game's unit     author extents      game extents
      skeleton_var03                424   0                     0.18 x 0.21 x 0.43  0.36 x 1.07 x 2.65
      crypt_railing_straight_4c     224   0                     0.02 x 0.45 x 0.45  0.68 x 1.15 x 8.34
      coffin_open                    64   4                     0.11 x 1.24 x 3.15  0.90 x 1.24 x 3.15
      brazier_floor_02               54   0                     0.25 x 1.78 x 1.79  2.11 x 2.30 x 2.30
      crypt_border_straight_2c       20   1                     0.83 x 1.12 x 3.95  0.83 x 1.12 x 4.01
      crypt_rock_01                   9   5                     4.06 x 5.12 x 5.42  4.02 x 4.95 x 5.54
      cryptsmooth_pillar_01           8   8                     1.59 x 1.60 x 5.97  (same)
      cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c        55  55                     0.00 x 8.00 x 8.00  (same)

  779 of 858 nodes are NOT the game unit their name resolves to.  A "railing" that is 0.45 x
  0.45 x 0.02 is a thin PLATE, not the game's 8.34-long railing; a "brazier" that is 1.78 x
  1.79 x 0.25 is a flat DISC.  They are the author's own proxy geometry, named after game units
  so that the level places the real ones.  Only the floors (55/55), the pillars (8/8) and most
  of the rocks (5/9) are the game's actual meshes.

  WHY THAT SCATTERS THE LEVEL.  The export is BAKED (9H.25: 0 of 858 nodes carry a transform),
  so the recovery has no transform to read and FITS the reference mesh to the node's geometry
  instead.  Fitting an 8.34-long railing onto a 0.45 plate is meaningless: the box ties on cube
  symmetries (which is where the 120 and 180 degree flips of 9H.26 come from) and the position,
  which is derived from the same fit, inherits the error.  The level then places the REAL units
  (because the name says so) at those wrong transforms - which is exactly "everything is
  scattered".  The room that worked was built the other way: 158 of its 206 nodes were the
  game's own meshes (the exact route), so the fit had something to fit.

  THE FIX IS NOT THE NAMING - the naming is the switch that gives native props, and it is doing
  what the author wants.  The fix is to NOT BAKE THE EXPORT: export from Blender without
  applying the transforms (do not Ctrl+A).  Then every node carries its own transform, the tool
  copies it instead of recovering it, and the proxy geometry only has to EXIST - it never has
  to match.  The tool now says this itself: recover_baked counts the nodes whose extents do not
  match the reference (25% per axis) and prints

      *** 779 node(s) ARE NOT THE GAME UNIT ***
      ... RE-EXPORT WITHOUT APPLYING THE TRANSFORMS (do not Ctrl+A)

  A level of proxies is a legitimate way to author, and a level of the game's own units is the
  path everything is verified on.  What does NOT work is proxies in a baked export, because the
  only information left is the geometry, and the geometry is not the unit.

  FOR THE AUTHOR'S OWN GEOMETRY IN GAME: that is a different feature - the level format places
  UNITS, so custom meshes have to ship as unit resources.  That is already solved and confirmed
  in game (Data/CUSTOM_ASSET_LOADING_FINDINGS.md #10, the complete working recipe: a custom unit
  spawned and rendered with a custom material, zero native overrides).  The tool would need to
  resolve the author's own unit paths for the names to reach them.

9H.26 "EVERYTHING IS SCATTERED" - WHAT IT MEASURED, AND ONE REAL BUG  *** Oct 2026 ***
================================================================================

  The author loaded the symmetric test arena (9H.25) and reported "super messed up, pretty much
  everything is scattered".  Measured against that run's capture:

      positions : 222 of 224 records join the engine's units at the offset (4,4,0), to 0.01
      rotations : 160 exact, 62 different
      angles between the record and the engine's value, over the 62:
                  180 deg x133, 120 deg x52, 109 x6, 90 x12, and a tail of props
      symmetry  : our records mirror 51/51, and the ENGINE'S OWN VALUES mirror 50/51

  180 and 120 are the give-away: those are CUBE SYMMETRIES.  A rectangular box is invariant
  under them, so a fit that only looks at extents cannot tell them apart, and the engine's
  value and ours are related by exactly one of them.

  THE BUG THIS FOUND (real, and now fixed).  recover_baked built its candidate orientations as
  `for axis in x,y,z: for angle in 0,90,180,270`, which yields TEN distinct matrices - identity,
  90/270 about the three face axes (6), 180 about the three face axes (3).  A cube's proper
  rotation group has TWENTY-FOUR; the missing fourteen are the 120/240 about the four body
  diagonals (8) and the 180 about the six edge axes (6) - exactly the angles measured above.
  The code's own comment claimed "twenty-four candidate orientations".  Fixed: the candidates
  are now generated as the 24 proper signed-permutation matrices.

  AND A SECOND ONE.  The vertex check that was supposed to out-vote the box ran per PART of a
  kind while a baked node carries ALL of them, so for the multi-part kinds (a railing is two
  coincident parts, a skeleton a dozen) it scored noise and the box decided.  It now unions the
  kind's parts and compares the node's whole cloud against the whole unit, over all 24
  candidates, with the clouds subsampled to 256 points so the O(n^2) distance stays cheap.

  WHAT THE FIX DID, HONESTLY: it changed the placement set (206 -> 220) and the file, but for
  the units present in both builds the recovered ROTATIONS did not change, and the match rate
  against the capture did not move (72% before and after).  So the tilt problem is NOT closed
  by these two fixes - they remove two ways for the fit to be wrong, but something else still
  decides the tilt.

  WHAT IS STILL OPEN, AND THE ONE QUESTION THAT SEPARATES THE CASES

    * the capture cannot be the oracle on its own: 9H.24.1 already showed the engine's stored
      value and what the author SEES disagree (the rotation ruler's three non-zero columns).
    * the ruler's rotations are CONSTRUCTED by the tool, not recovered, and they look right.
      Every reported problem has been on a RECOVERED unit.  That is the strongest signal in
      the whole file: the writer and the format are fine, the recovery is what is wrong.
    * a character (skeleton_var03) is ASYMMETRIC, so its tilt is visible: if the skeletons in
      the arena are standing up, the tilt is being applied correctly and "scattered" is
      something else; if they are lying on their sides, the tilt is being lost, and that is
      the classic single-frame bug of 9H.22 coming back for the box route.
    * the minimal test that settles it in one look: ONE kind, eight units, nothing else -
      four lying flat at yaw 0/90/180/270, four rotated 90 degrees about a horizontal axis.
      If the standing ones come back flat (or the flat ones standing), the tilt is lost.

================================================================================

  The author re-exported the test arena with deliberate symmetry in it.  It is 858 nodes,
  still a BAKED export (0 of 858 nodes carry a transform), so the build is the recovery route:

      python3 scripts/level_from_glb.py test_arena_03.glb <sample> out.level \\
          --scratch --rotation-ruler --reference crypt_arena01.glb --reference
          crypt_arena02.glb --reference crypt_arena03.glb --reference
          room_crypt_floor_01_adventure.glb

      858 mesh nodes -> 206 placed, 0 dropped, 8 kinds, 652 mesh parts merged away
      68 recovered vertex-for-vertex, 790 from bounding boxes
      yaw-only (a box cannot say more): skeleton_var03 x424, crypt_railing_straight_4c x224,
      coffin_open x64, brazier_floor_02 x54, crypt_border_straight_2c x20, crypt_rock_01 x4
      224 records = 206 placements + 16 ruler + spawn + skydome, 19,448 bytes

  SYMMETRY IS A GROUND TRUTH WE CAN CHECK OFFLINE, and that is the point of the export.  If a
  layout mirrors in the plane x = c, a reflection takes a yaw t to -t, so every mirrored pair
  must come back with mirrored yaws.  scripts/check_level_symmetry.py finds the centre that
  pairs the most placements (x = 0.30 here) and tests each pair:

      51 mirrored pairs, 51 correctly mirrored, 0 not.

  So the recovery's HANDEDNESS is right, on every pair the author built - which is a much
  stronger statement than "the level loads".  It also means the axis composition F^-1 * R_node
  * F' does not break a mirror relationship, so the two-frame handling (9H.22) is doing its job.

  The one apparent exception in the first run was a rotation-ruler fixture (a diagnostic row at
  the -x edge) pairing with an unrelated unit of the same kind; excluding the fixtures by index
  rather than by name removed it.  Fixtures are not authored content and must not be counted.

  DEPLOYED through the normal route (mod_bundle_builder.py --flat into the loader's
  mods/bundles/CustomRunLevel), manifest size 19,448 - the new level, not the old 16,928 one.

  WHAT THE AUTHOR'S LOOK WILL SETTLE
    * if the two halves mirror in game, then the records are right in the engine's frame too,
      and the "wrong units" reports belong to the earlier builds (9H.23.6's mirror bug and the
      pre-branch builds), not to this one;
    * if they do not, the mirror breaks at named units, and those are the recovery's remaining
      error sites - which is exactly the list to hand back.
    * the rotation ruler's three non-zero columns are still the open question from 9H.24.1: the
      capture says the engine stores 180/250.5/289.5 for what we wrote as 90/180/270.

================================================================================
9H.28 THE AUTHOR'S OWN UNITS: A LEVEL PLACES UNITS, SO THE MESH HAS TO BE ONE
  *** Oct 2026 ***
================================================================================

  A .level carries no geometry.  Its 72-byte records hold a template hash, a material hash, a
  position and a rotation, and the engine resolves that hash to a UNIT resource and
  instantiates it.  So a level authored from the author's own meshes only works if those meshes
  SHIP AS UNITS - which is what the game itself does, and what
  Data/CUSTOM_ASSET_LOADING_FINDINGS.md #10 already proved in game (a custom unit rendered with
  its own material, no native override).

  THE GROUPING RULE, as the author stated it and the engine agrees: A NODE'S NAME IS THE UNIT.
  `Floor`, `Floor.001`, ... `Floor.008` are ONE unit resource placed nine times.  Blender's
  `.001`/`#123` suffixes are stripped exactly as level_from_glb already stripped them for game
  units, so the two tools group identically.

  scripts/build_own_units.py (new) builds one unit per base name: it takes the mesh from the
  FIRST node of the group, rotates it into the engine's frame, encodes it with gltf_to_unit.py,
  and SPLICES it into a native template - keeping the template's trailer (collision actors,
  flow data, the 32-byte footer whose material hash is the binding).  The template choice is
  the physics and the material, which is why a floor tile is built from a floor tile.

  THE FRAME IS MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED.  The vertices have to end up in engine space (Z-up)
  because the record's rotation is applied to them at run time.  The rotation is the one that
  takes the export's mesh up - the flat meshes' averaged vertex normal - onto engine +Z:

      +Y   (every normalised Blender +Y-up export)   +90 about X   (x, y, z) -> (x, -z, y)
      -Y   (Y-up but upside down; the old Base.glb)  -90 about X
      +Z   (the mesh data is already engine-space)     0
      -Z   (engine-space but upside down)            180
  `--mesh-frame auto|y-up|engine` overrides the measurement when there is no flat mesh to
  measure.  It was +90 hard-coded before; on THIS export the measurement picks +90 as well, and
  the three units rebuild BYTE-FOR-BYTE identical to the shipped ones (1422 / 9582 / 1164296
  bytes), which is the regression check that the detection is not a behaviour change.

  THE EXPORT AND THE UNITS THAT CAME OUT OF IT (test_arena_03.glb, 12 mesh nodes, no hierarchy)

      node(s)          mesh                          unit resource built from it
      Floor x9         9 verts, 8 tris, 8 x 0 x 8    units/custom/duckomp_room/floor  1422 B
      Stair x2         106 verts, 2 prims, 98 tris   units/custom/duckomp_room/stair  9582 B
      Medieval Crane   19156 verts, 13344 tris       units/custom/duckomp_room/crane  1.16 MB

  Templates, and the material that comes with each (the footer u64 at len-16, named through
  Data/hash_database.json - all three are the same crypt-stone material, 0x04d8b35043c51085):

      units/custom/duckomp_room/floor  <- 0x6369abda98cf91a5  cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c
      units/custom/duckomp_room/stair  <- 0xc512fd67e8213eec  crypt_stairs_low_4c
      units/custom/duckomp_room/crane  <- 0x432ffd51ba8d0fc3  crypt_pillar_01

  NOTE ON THE TEMPLATE FOOTER: all three footer hashes came out 0x04d8b35043c51085, i.e. the
  templates' own binding, NOT the author's material.  The engine resolves it, which is what
  makes the geometry render; the record's own material field is written 0 ("no override",
  never a NULL lookup) for all 12 placements.

  level_from_glb.py takes `--own-unit BASE=PATH` (repeatable): a node whose base name is BASE
  resolves to that unit path instead of a game unit, and the path is hashed with the same
  murmur64 the bundle builder uses, so the level's template hash and the bundle's resource
  name hash agree by construction - they were checked against each other in the DEPLOYED bundle
  (9H.30).

  SHIPPED: scripts/arena_mod/custom_run_mod.json now carries the level plus the three units in
  ONE package (a package onlines only when every resource in it completes, so a level whose
  units live somewhere else would load to nothing).

================================================================================

================================================================================
9H.29 THE ROTATION BRANCH, APPLIED TWICE: EVERY NON-YAW PLACEMENT CAME OUT INVERTED
  *** Oct 2026 ***
================================================================================

  WHAT WAS SEEN.  The first own-unit build (12 placements, an un-baked export) decoded as:

      Floor x9          quat (0, 0, 0, -1)                 identity        looks right
      Medieval Crane    quat (0, 0, +0.8108, -0.5853)      251.6 deg about -Z
      Stair             quat (-0.7071, 0, 0, -0.7071)      +90 deg about X
      Stair.001         quat (-0.5, -0.5, +0.5, -0.5)      120 deg about (+1, +1, -1)

  and the suspicion was that all twelve should be IDENTITY because "every node in the export has
  translation only".  MEASURED, that premise is false - this export carries three node
  rotations, and the build reports them:

      Medieval Crane   (0, +0.8108, 0, +0.5853)    108.36 deg about Y in the file
      Stair            (-0.7071, 0, 0, +0.7071)    -90 deg about X
      Stair.001        (-0.5, +0.5, +0.5, +0.5)    120 deg about (-1, +1, +1)/sqrt(3)

  So 9 identity records and 3 rotated ones is the CORRECT shape of the answer, the earlier
  "translation only" measurement belongs to an older export, and the only question was whether
  the 3 rotated records hold the right rotation.  They did not.

  THE RULE, and where a record's rotation comes from.  The engine renders the unit's ENGINE mesh
  M_e transformed by R_record; the file renders its mesh M_file transformed by R_node; and
  M_e = F * M_file for BOTH a native unit in a Y-up file and an own unit built by
  build_own_units.py (both are engine-space copies of the same geometry).  So

      R_record = F * R_node * F^-1        (F = the file -> engine change, +90 about X here)

  which is what to_engine_rotation computes from deg_pos = +90 (measured from the layout's world
  up) and deg_mesh = -90 (measured from the meshes' normals).  Against that rule, the records
  above are the INVERSE of the authored rotation in all three cases.

  THE BUG.  to_engine_rotation put its result into the engine's branch by negating ONLY xyz and
  leaving w positive.  That is the right xyz for an engine that rebuilds w = -|w|, but it is NOT
  a canonical quaternion, and write_scratch then applied the record-branch rule
  (`_record_branch`: negate ALL FOUR when w > 0) on top of it - so the second application undid
  the first and put the sign back.  (x, y, z, w) and (-x, -y, -z, w) are NOT the same rotation
  (the second is the inverse); only the full negation is.

  WHY IT HAD NEVER SHOWN.  Every earlier build was BAKED, so every placement went through
  recover_baked -> _record_branch exactly ONCE, which is correct.  The rotation ruler builds its
  own quaternions and gets the same single application - correct (9H.23.7).  The one path that
  was wrong is the one that had never been used: an UN-BAKED export - which is precisely the
  export 9H.27 demands.  And it is invisible in the file: the nine floors are correct either way
  because their node rotation IS the identity, so the branch has nothing to flip.  "The floors
  look right" was never evidence about this bug.

  THE FIX, and the check that makes it unable to come back.  to_engine_rotation negates ALL FOUR
  components, so its output is canonical (w < 0) and _record_branch is a no-op on it; the
  idempotence is now stated in both docstrings, and the verbatim path (which already negated all
  four) stops being double-flipped with it.  write_scratch also AUDITS every placement before
  writing - it rebuilds the rotation the engine will construct from the stored branch and
  compares it with the orientation the node asked for (`_quat_angle_deg`), prints

      rotation: 12 placement(s): 9 identity, 3 rotated; the stored branch reproduces
