                the authored orientation on every one (worst 0.000 deg)
                1 x quat (-0.0000, -0.0000, -0.8108, -0.5853)  = 108.4 deg about (0, 0, +1)
                1 x quat (+0.5000, +0.5000, -0.5000, -0.5000)  = 120.0 deg about (-0.577, -0.577, +0.577)
                1 x quat (+0.7071, -0.0000, -0.0000, -0.7071)  =  90.0 deg about (-1, 0, 0)

  and REFUSES the build if any record is off by more than 1e-3 degrees.

  VERIFIED END-TO-END FROM THE LEVEL FILE - decode the record, rebuild the engine's quaternion
  q = (x, y, z, -sqrt(1 - |xyz|^2)), compare with the authored rotation:

      Floor x9  0.000 deg    Medieval Crane  0.000 deg    Stair  0.015 deg    Stair.001  0.000 deg
      worst over the 12 placements: 0.0150 deg  (float32 rounding in the file)

  THE ONE THING STILL OPEN, AND IT IS THE AUTHOR'S CALL.  Those three rotations are the author's
  own Blender objects: a crane at 108.36 degrees and two stairs at -90 and +120 degrees.  The
  records now reproduce the FILE exactly.  If a piece still looks rotated oddly in game, the
  number to change is in Blender, not in the tool.


================================================================================
9H.30 THE SPAWN ON OUR OWN FLOOR, AND THE BUILD THAT SHIPS  *** Oct 2026 ***
================================================================================

  OPEN ITEM: THE SPAWN MARKER.  The first own-unit build printed

      spawn  : WARNING - no floors/ among our units; the spawn marker goes to the origin

  The test was `'floors/' in path` - the corpus' own directory (blocks/d01_crypt/floors/...).  An
  own unit is `units/custom/duckomp_room/floor`, which has neither the slash nor the plural, so
  nothing nominated a spawn and the marker landed at (0, 0, 0).  The nine tiles sit at z = -0.18,
  so the origin is 0.18 ABOVE the tile surface and on the centre tile - survivable, but an
  accident rather than a decision.

  THE RULE NOW, measured over all 280 unit kinds the shipped corpus places:

      * `floors/` anywhere in the path      - the old test, kept so no shipped level moves;
      * a path SEGMENT named `floor`/`floors` - catches units/custom/duckomp_room/floor;
      * a final segment STARTING WITH `floor`.

      old rule: 21 kinds      new rule: 21 kinds, plus ours      lost: none, gained: none

  so no shipped behaviour moves (including gameobjects/procedural_floors/entrance_blocker, which
  the substring test caught).  A plain `'floor' in path` was REJECTED: it also catches
  gameobjects/deathplane/deathfloor, which is a kill volume.

  The marker now goes to the median floor position per axis, which for nine 8-unit tiles is the
  CENTRE TILE's centre AND its surface height:

      spawn  : our own player_spawn_marker at (0.29, 0.01, -0.18) - the median floor tile's
               centre, ON the tile surface

  `--spawn-at X,Y,Z` places it by hand when the median is not what is wanted.  The tiles are at
  x = 8.29 / 0.29 / -7.71 and y = 8.01 / 0.01 / -7.99 (8 apart), so (0.29, 0.01) is the middle
  tile - and the layout's own symmetry agrees: 8 mirrored pairs, 8 correctly mirrored, 0 not
  (scripts/check_level_symmetry.py).

  THE BUILD THAT SHIPPED

      scripts/arena_mod/custom_duckomp_room.level      14 records = 12 placements + spawn +
                                                       skydome, 1808 bytes
      scripts/arena_mod/units/units_custom_duckomp_room_{floor,stair,crane}.unit
                                                       1422 / 9582 / 1164296 bytes
      python3 scripts/mod_bundle_builder.py scripts/arena_mod/custom_run_mod.json --flat \
          -o <game>/mods/bundles/CustomRunLevel

  manifest.json before -> after: resource_count 2 -> 5; the level resource's size 20624 -> 1808
  (20624 was the previous donor-body level - a different file entirely); the package resource
  25 -> 85 bytes; three new unit resources.  bundle.ini ResourceCount agrees with the manifest
  (5), which the C++ loader requires or it skips the bundle.

  VERIFIED BY DECODING THE DEPLOYED BUNDLE (not the builder's own output): sig 0xF0000004,
  1,177,713 bytes unpacked / 275,819 compressed, the five payloads packed tight (consumed ==
  size, no padding), the TOC agreeing with every payload header, and

      all 12 placement template hashes ARE unit resources in this package
      (0x6892a66dda35e093 floor x9, 0x319ab70e4a95accd crane, 0xb24202c709ee3127 stair x2)
      the level's other two records are the spawn marker and the skeleton's skydome, both native
      the three units' footer material is 0x04d8b35043c51085, the templates' crypt-stone

  That agreement is the one that matters: the level places units BY HASH, so a hash that is not
  in the loaded package is a unit that does not exist.

  DEPLOYED to <game>/mods/bundles/CustomRunLevel (the previous folder backed up to
  /tmp/CustomRunLevel.backup-<time>).  The CustomRun Lua that exposes the level
  (mods/functions/CustomRun: the custom_run menu button -> layouts/custom_duckomp.lua ->
  modules/d01_hub_room/custom_duckomp_room) is already in the game dir and byte-identical to the
  repo copy.

  WHAT ONLY THE AUTHOR CAN SETTLE: whether the crane and the two stairs stand and face the way
  they were built - they carry the export's own rotations (9H.29) - and whether the geometry
  renders with the templates' crypt-stone material, which is what the spliced trailers bring.

  AFTER THE FIRST LOAD there is a second, independent check available: the engine writes its own
  capture of every level it loads to <game>/binaries/levels/<level path>.json, so

      python3 scripts/compare_level_capture.py scripts/arena_mod/custom_duckomp_room.level \
          <game>/binaries/levels/modules_d01_hub_room_custom_duckomp_room.json

  joins the engine's own unit transforms to our records on (rounded position, template hash) and
  says whether the positions and the rotations agree.  The capture is NOT the last word on what
  is RENDERED (9H.24.1: it reported 180/250.5/289.5 for what was written as 90/180/270), so it is
  corroboration - the author's eyes decide.




9H.31 A UNIT BODY MUST DECLARE AS MANY MESHES AS THE TRAILER IT WEARS  *** Oct 2026 ***
     --- the load crash, the two mesh bugs behind it, and the whole level -> unit path ---

THE CRASH.  access read at 00000008, fault RVA 0x00270259 (`push [eax+ecx+8]`, eax =
[[ebp+0x2c]+0x1e4], which is NULL), reached from 0x004309f3 (the per-unit pass FUN_00430440) and
0x00429e90 / 0x00429d83 (the World.load_level region).  Deterministic: every load of our
12-placement level with our own units died there, and the engine never even wrote its own level
capture - so the crash is BEFORE the capture, not after the menu.

THE BISECT (four game launches, driven by a temporary AutoLoadTest harness that hooks
ScreenMainMenu.update and calls Game:create_network_game + Game:change_state_to_game):

  1. the same 14 records with the three kinds resolving to NATIVE crypt units
     (cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c / crypt_stairs_low_4c / crypt_pillar_01)     -> LOADS
  2. OUR floor unit placed 9 times, native stair + native pillar           -> LOADS
  3. OUR crane unit placed once, native floor + native stair               -> CRASHES (same RVA)
  4. OUR crane MESH wearing the FLOOR template's trailer                   -> LOADS

Run 1 cleared the level file itself (records, rotations, spawn, skydome, the bundle plumbing:
a level whose 3 kinds are native units loads out of OUR package).  Run 2 cleared our floor.  Run
3 named the crane.  Run 4 named the CAUSE: the body and the crane's own template differed in
nothing but the trailer, and the trailer that loaded came from a ONE-mesh template.

WHAT THE FILES SAY.  A .unit body is

      u32 0x00010090
      u32 mesh_count
      mesh_count x [ vertex buffer | 8 bytes | count/stride/elements | elements | 12 bytes |
                     index count/size | indices | shapes | bbox | material list ]
      trailer      (collision actors, flow data, the 32-byte footer whose material hash is the
                    unit's material binding)

and THE TRAILER IS SERIALIZED FOR THE MESH LIST OF THE UNIT IT WAS TAKEN FROM.  Our builder
splices a generated ONE-mesh body onto a template's trailer, and the three templates differ:

      0x6369abda98cf91a5  blocks/d01_crypt/floors/cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c  1 mesh    670 B trailer
      0xc512fd67e8213eec  blocks/d01_crypt/stairs/crypt_stairs_low_4c      5 meshes 2858 B trailer
      0x432ffd51ba8d0fc3  blocks/d01_crypt/pillars/crypt_pillar_01         2 meshes 11287 B trailer

  136 of the 582 native units in scripts/.unit orig are multi-mesh (up to 36), so this is not an
  edge case: the floor (1) loaded, the stair (5) and the crane (2) could not.

THE CODE, OUT OF THE BINARY (Ghidra 12.1.4, project ~/tools/ghproj, script in
/tmp/dump345/unit_path.py):

  FUN_00422760  the .unit deserializer - it is the function that writes/checks 0x00010090 and
                then reads the fields in file order.
  FUN_00422fd0  the constructor that ZEROES the mesh list: [x+0x1dc] count, [x+0x1e0],
                [x+0x1e4] array - three more arrays are zeroed the same way (0x1b0, 0x1ec).
  FUN_004272b0  the append into that array (reads [x+0x1e4], writes it back, pushes the count).
  FUN_00430440  the per-unit pass: the 4-byte-per-unit pointer table, the 72-byte records, the
                transform/render/lighting block.
  FUN_00670190  THE FAULT.  It is the mesh -> material resolver, and its own strings say so:
                    "MeshObject"
                    "Failed looking up material: '%s' in material manager for mesh '%s' in
                     unit '%s'"
                Body:  uVar1 = [X+0x1dc]                    (the mesh COUNT)
                       resize/grow the instance's material array to uVar1
                       for i in 0..uVar1:
                           FUN_00629fe0(*(int *)([X+0x1e4] + 8 + i*0x10))
                                                            (the mesh object at +8 of the
                                                             16-byte element)
                With a body that declared ONE mesh and a trailer written for two or five, that
                list is left size-non-zero / array-NULL, so the first element read is
                [NULL+8] = access read at 00000008, RVA 0x00270259.  Exactly the signature.

THE FIX.  splice_into_template() in scripts/glb_to_template_unit.py keeps the counts EQUAL: when
the generated body has fewer mesh blocks than the template, the block is REPLICATED to the
template's count - a mesh block is self-contained (its own vertex buffer, elements, indices,
shapes, bbox and material list), so N copies parse exactly like one, and N is what the trailer
was serialized against.  `--no-duplicate-meshes` refuses instead, which forces a template that
is already one mesh.  build_own_units.py prints both numbers, e.g.

    template : 0xc512fd67e8213eec.unit (24253 bytes, 5 mesh(es), trailer 2858 bytes)
    Stair    -> units/custom/duckomp_room/stair  2 placement(s)  166 verts  158 tris
                55046 bytes  (5 mesh block(s), 2 primitive(s): 106+98 60+60)

  Rebuilt: floor 1422 B - BYTE-IDENTICAL to the crashing build, because a one-mesh template
  needs no replication.  That is the regression check.  Stair 36446 -> 55046 B, crane 2,317,297
  B.  Deployed, launched, and the level loads: 12 units in the engine's capture, no exception.

THE SECOND BUG, FOUND WHILE AUDITING THE MESH.  build_own_units.py read
`meshes[node.mesh].primitives[0]` and dropped every other primitive.  A glTF mesh with N
primitives is N MATERIAL GROUPS over one surface - the engine's own model is one material group
per mesh block, which is why native units are multi-mesh at all - and the stair's mesh is 2
(106 verts / 98 tris + 60 verts / 60 tris, two materials).  A third of the stair was missing from
the unit.  All primitives are now concatenated with their index offsets and the build reports
the breakdown.  Floors (1 primitive) and crane (1) are unaffected.

HOW A LEVEL PLACES A UNIT - the whole path, so the next custom unit does not have to be
re-derived (this is the answer to "find out everything about levels and loading their units"):

  World.load_level(path, position, rotation)      the Lua binding, FUN_004f2520
      -> FUN_00428a50   the delegate: allocates 0x208, calls the loader with the path, then
                        pushes the Level onto the world's level list (FUN_0042f3a0 follows)
      -> FUN_0042b6b0   the loader proper: builds the Level, walks the unit table - ONE 72-BYTE
                        RECORD PER PLACEMENT, template hash + position + rotation - and recurses
                        per sub-level.  It creates no physics.
      -> per placement: the record's hash is looked up as a RESOURCE, so the unit must come from
                        a package that is loaded (that is what our bundle registers, 9H.28)
      -> FUN_00430440   the per-unit pass: the 4-byte-per-unit pointer table (its entries are
                        SELF-RELATIVE and memcopied - getting the count wrong is the 0x5FEA5A
                        crash of 9H.23), the transform, the render/lighting block
      -> FUN_00670190   mesh -> material resolution, once per mesh of the unit's mesh list
                        (this is the function that dies when the list is inconsistent)
      -> FUN_004e7ed0 / FUN_0045a890  actor instantiation from the trailer's actor table
                        ([unit+0xbc] count, [unit+0xc4] array): this is what gives a placed unit
                        its collision - a level never carries physics of its own

  A record carries NO GEOMETRY.  Hash, position, rotation, and the unit resource does the rest -
  which is why the body/trailer agreement above is load-critical and why the units must ship
  beside the level.

THE PLACEMENT ORIENTATION, MEASURED.  For every mesh node of the author's export the tool's rule
is (engine and Blender are both Z-up, so the frame change is R_x(90) from glTF's Y-up):

      engine position P  = R_x(+90) . t_file
      engine rotation R  = R_x(+90) . R_node . R_x(-90)
      our unit mesh      = R_x(+90) . v_file

so the rendered world W = P + R . v_engine reproduces the node's own world placement, which is
what level_from_glb.build() audits per placement (ORIENT_TOL_DEG = 0.5) and what the rotation
ruler proved.  The numbers for THIS level (floor tile surface at z = -0.185):

  Floor x9        identity            nine tiles, flat, at z = -0.185
  Medieval Crane  108.35 about -Z     local Z (9.977 tall) -> +Z; stands on the floor
  Stair           90 about -X         run (2.558) -> +Y, width (1.0) -> +Z: FLAT, bottom
                                      z = -0.163, i.e. 0.022 ABOVE the tile surface
  Stair.001       120 about (-1,-1,1) run (2.558) -> -X, width (1.0) -> +Z: FLAT, but YAWED 90
                                      against Stair - its 4-unit length runs along +Y instead
                                      of +X, and it climbs towards -X

  THE AUTHOR'S EXPORT SAYS THAT, AND ONLY THAT.  Stair.001's node rotation is (-0.5, 0.5, 0.5,
  0.5) - 120 degrees about the (-1,1,1) DIAGONAL - where Stair carries the clean (-0.7071, 0, 0,
  0.7071), i.e. -90 about X.  The two stairs are not parallel in the file, and the tool copies
  what the file says on purpose (worst orientation error 0.000 deg over all 12 placements).  A
  diagonal-axis rotation of exactly 120 degrees is the signature of a trackball/gizmo drag or of
  two typed Euler fields; the fix belongs in Blender (set Stair.001's rotation, e.g. -90 about X
  like Stair, plus whatever yaw is wanted), not in the level file.

  THE ENGINE'S CAPTURE IS NOT EVIDENCE FOR THIS.  binaries/levels/<level>.json reports different
  rotations for exactly the three rotated placements - 109.47 where the stair is written as 90,
  97.9 where the crane is written as 108.35, 81.79 where the stair is written as 120 - while the
  nine identity floors agree.  9H.24.1 already recorded that artifact (180 / 250.5 / 289.5 for
  what was written as 90 / 180 / 270).  The author's eyes settle it: the crane and the first
  stair stand exactly as authored, so the capture holds a derived value, not the render
  transform.  Use it to corroborate POSITIONS and unit hashes, never rotations.

STATE AND TOOLING.  scripts/build_own_units.py (--mesh-frame auto|y-up|engine,
--no-duplicate-meshes), its splice helper in glb_to_template_unit.py, scripts/check_rotation_rule.py
(15 cases), scripts/check_level_symmetry.py, scripts/level_decode.py, scripts/compare_level_capture.py,
and scripts/check_deployed_bundle.py (new): it unpacks the DEPLOYED blob, checks the TOC against
every payload header, checks the resource count against manifest.json AND bundle.ini, checks that
every placement hash in the level is a unit in the package, and checks every unit's mesh_count
against the native template whose trailer it wears - the invariant whose violation was this
crash.  On the shipped bundle it reports:

    sig 0xF0000004, 5 resources, 2,376,178 bytes unpacked, the walk ending exactly at the end
    units/custom/duckomp_room/crane  mesh_count 2  trailer 11287 B  (template declares 2)
    units/custom/duckomp_room/floor  mesh_count 1  trailer   670 B  (template declares 1)
    units/custom/duckomp_room/stair  mesh_count 5  trailer  2858 B  (template declares 5)
    the level payload is 1808 bytes, 14 records (12 of them our units, 2 native)

The deployed bundle is <game>/mods/bundles/CustomRunLevel (5 resources, crane 2,317,297 B, file
545,799 B) and the level captures are the engine's own.  The temporary harness
<game>/mods/functions/AutoLoadTest/ (arms ScreenMainMenu.update, calls Game:create_network_game +
change_state_to_game at frame 240) is what made four launches into four experiments - REMOVE IT
(delete or rename that folder) before the shipped state is called clean.

9H.32 THE ROTATION'S TWO HALVES, AND WHY THE CAPTURE CANNOT SETTLE IT  *** Oct 2026 ***
     --- the author's "w 0.5 -> 1.0", the record's dual-purpose first float, four test builds ---

WHAT THE AUTHOR FOUND, AND WHAT IT IS.  "Stair.001's W is changed from 0.5 to 1.0 by the engine;
when I put 1.0 in Blender it matches what the game shows."  The 72-byte unit record stores the
rotation in TWO places:

    offset  0   a float that is the quaternion's w   - and, in record 0 ONLY, the load's COUNT
    offset 60   the rotation vector, 3 floats (xyz)
    offset 72   (the next record) - see below for why this matters

  write_scratch (scripts/level_from_glb.py) says it plainly: "the record holds xyz of the branch
  the engine rebuilds w=-|w| from", and it forces `records[0]['flag'] = 0.0` because the loader
  reads the unit table's own start - record 0's offset-0 field - as a byte COUNT, allocates that
  many bytes and writes one byte per item.  A float there is read as a count: 0.5 becomes
  1,056,964,608.  BOTH false trails of this session were that mistake: two test builds that wrote
  a w into record 0 crashed at RVA 0x88344 (FUN_00488340, a bare `return *param_1` on a garbage
  pointer) - not a floor bug, not a spawn bug, the count.

WHAT THE FOUR TEST BUILDS SHOWED (each one game launch, level patched directly):
  * four floor tiles given four unit quaternions about Z (60/120/180/240)   -> four DIFFERENT
    rotations in game, i.e. the engine uses each record's own data, NOT one forced value.
  * three stair records encoding +90 about X (A), and -90 about X twice with different
    (vector, w) pairs (B, C) -> "all rotated weirdly and differently" per the author.

WHY THE ENGINE'S CAPTURE CANNOT DECIDE IT.  For A/B/C the capture reported A and B as the SAME
rotation (0.00 deg apart) and C 70.53 deg away - normalize(vector, w=1.0) exactly.  That pattern
is not consistent with the engine honouring (vector, w) (which would make B and C identical, the
two encodings of -90 about X, and A their mirror).  9H.24.1 recorded the same class of artifact
(180 / 250.5 / 289.5 for records written as 90 / 180 / 270), and the capture's numbers for the
other three placements each equal normalize(record.xyz, the NEXT record's first float) exactly -
i.e. the capture's rotation is a DERIVED value, one foot in the next record.  Use the capture for
POSITIONS and unit hashes; never for rotations.  The author's eyes are the instrument.

PRACTICAL ANSWERS.
  * Blender's rotation MODE does not matter: the glTF exporter writes node rotations as
    quaternions whatever the object's rotation_mode is, so switching the N-panel to XYZ changes
    nothing in the file.  What the pipeline copies is the resulting ORIENTATION.
  * The crane "just works" because its node rotation is a clean single-axis yaw (108.35 about +Y
    in the file = 108.35 about +Z in the engine).  Stair is a clean -90 about X.  Stair.001 is
    120 degrees about the (-1,-1,1) DIAGONAL - flat, but yawed 90 against Stair, and the
    signature of a trackball drag or two typed Euler fields.
  * If a node's authored rotation is wrong, the fix is in Blender.  A level-side override is the
    alternative (a --set-rotation flag), and if the engine turns out to force the w slot, the
    writer compensates by pre-scaling the vector (v' = tan(theta/2) * axis) - neither is built
    yet because the A/B/C observation decides which one is needed.

9H.33 [RETRACTED - see 9H.34] "THE RECORD'S ROTATION IS A ROTATION VECTOR"
     *** This section was WRONG.  It was written from an in-game A/B test that could not
     distinguish the two readings on a symmetric mesh.  The decompile in 9H.34 shows the engine
     builds a NORMALISED QUATERNION (x,y,z,w) from four floats, and the corpus agrees with it and
     not with this.  Kept only so the wrong turn is on record. ***

     --- what was believed, briefly: the 3 floats as axis*tan(theta/2), q = normalize(v, 1.0) ---

THE RULE.  A 72-byte unit record holds THREE floats for its rotation (offsets 60/64/68), and the
engine builds the quaternion as

        q = normalize(vector, 1.0)

so the three floats are a ROTATION VECTOR, axis * tan(theta/2) - not a quaternion's vector part.
For a unit quaternion (x, y, z, w) a record therefore wants (x, y, z) / w.  Under this encoding a
90-degree rotation is a unit vector, 120 degrees is sqrt(3) long, 108.35 is 1.3854, and 180 is
infinite.  The offset-0 float plays no part in the rotation; on the first record it is the byte
count the loader allocates, which write_scratch pins to 0.0 (9H.23.3).

WHAT WE WERE WRITING, AND WHAT IT LOOKED LIKE.  We stored the quaternion's vector part, which the
engine then read as tan(theta/2): every rotation rendered at 2*atan(sin(theta/2)) - a SHRUNKEN
angle about the SAME axis.  scripts/check_rotation_encoding.py prints the table:

        90 degrees as written  ->  70.53   (19.47 off)
       108.35 (the crane)      ->  78.07   (30.28 off)
       120 (the author's diagonal stair) -> 81.79 (38.21 off)   <-- the tilt in the screenshot
       170                     ->  89.78   (80.22 off)

  Identity is the one rotation both encodings agree on (the vector is 0), so nine floors and every
  unrotated prop were ALWAYS correct - which is why this hid for so long, and why the author's
  original observation, "the engine changes w from 0.5 to 1.0, and 1.0 in Blender matches the
  game", was the true description of the bug rather than a symptom of it.

THE PROOF, IN GAME.  Four stair records were written with ONE intended orientation (stair #1's,
-90 about X) in four encodings:

        a  vector (+0.7071, 0, 0)   offset0 -0.7071   (a quaternion's vector part, canonical)
        b  vector (-0.7071, 0, 0)   offset0 +0.7071   (the same rotation, the other branch)
        c  vector (-1.0,     0, 0)  offset0 +1.0      (the rotation vector of -90 about X)
        d  vector (+1.0,     0, 0)  offset0 +1.0      (the rotation vector of +90 about X)

  The author's verdict: "the back left and back middle planes ... look correctly rotated as stairs"
  - i.e. c and d (the rotation-vector forms) are right and a and b (the quaternion forms) are not.
  That rejects "the engine reads (vector, offset0)", rejects "offset0 is the w", and leaves exactly
  q = normalize(vector, 1.0).  It also explains why the crane - a 108.35 yaw, rendered 30 degrees
  short - still looked plausible: a big prop with no reference direction.

THE FIX (scripts/resource_tools/level_tools.py).  record_rotation_vector() divides by w; the
packer _encode_entity() writes that, preferring a decoded record's own three floats when present
so a round trip stays byte-exact; quat_from_record_vector() is the engine's rebuild, used by
_parse_entity() so every report now shows the rotation the ENGINE will build; and both build
audits (write_scratch's per-placement audit and build()'s --scratch orientation check) now measure
the authored orientation against the ENGINE'S REBUILD of the record's floats - so "the level is
what the author authored" is verified on the bytes, per placement, on every build (worst 0.000 deg
on all 12).  Exactly 180 degrees cannot be written in this form (tan 90 is infinite) and gets a
1e6 escape value, which the engine normalises back to 180.

  The 9H.29 "canonical branch" work is moot under this encoding: q and -q give the same (x,y,z)/w,
  so there is no branch to canonicalise.  That whole thread was chasing the symptom of this one.

IMPACT.  Every level our tools wrote before today carried shrunken rotations - the tumbling custom
levels of 9H.22/9H.26 included.  The record vector for the author's level is now
9H.34 THE TRANSFORM BUILDER, DECOMPILED  *** Oct 2026 ***
     --- what the engine does with a record's rotation, read out of gauntlet.exe ---

MEASURED IN THE BINARY.  The per-unit pass FUN_00430440 gets its record pointer from the world's
per-unit instance array and hands the transform builder three of its members:

    iVar13 = *(int *)(param_1 + 8) + i * 0x48 + 0xb0;        ; 0x48 = 72 bytes per unit
    FUN_0042db10(out, iVar13 + 0xc, iVar13, iVar13 + 0x1c)   ; (out, quaternion, position, scale)
      -> FUN_0042d7f0(out, quaternion, position, scale)

and FUN_0042d7f0's arithmetic is a NORMALISED QUATERNION to matrix, in the ordinary (x, y, z, w)
order, with the w read as the FOURTH float:

    fVar5 = x*x + y*y + z*z + w*w;                 if (fVar5 <= eps) -> identity matrix
    k = 2.0 / fVar5;
    m00 = 1 - k(y*y + z*z)   m01 = k(x*y + w*z)   m02 = k(x*z - w*y)
    m10 = k(x*y - w*z)       m11 = 1 - k(x*x + z*z)   m12 = k(y*z + w*x)
    m20 = k(x*z + w*y)       m21 = k(y*z - w*x)   m22 = 1 - k(x*x + y*y)
    (then the translation is copied from the position pointer and m33 = 1)

So: FOUR floats, normalised, in (x, y, z, w) order - NOT a rotation vector, NOT a tangent-half
encoding, NOT three floats with anything derived.  FUN_0042db10 is called from exactly two places:
FUN_00430440 (the per-unit pass, the 72-byte unit records) and FUN_0042b6b0 (the loader), where the
same builder is handed a 56-byte placement record's own fields:

    puVar14 = (uint *)(*(int *)(param_1[2] + 0x78) + iStack_104 + param_1[2]);   ; 0x38 stride
    FUN_0042db10(out, puVar14 + 7, puVar14 + 4, puVar14 + 0xb);                  ; bytes 28, 16, 44

- the SHIPPED 56-byte placement records hold a COMPLETE FOUR-FLOAT QUATERNION at byte 28, with the
position at 16 and the scale at 44 (which is what level_decode's comment on that table said, and
what the builder's argument list now confirms).

MEASURED FROM THE CORPUS.  The game's own six-record level (assets/exports-samples/
samples/level_24abcdf4b1fb4d4a.level) reads as clean grid yaws under (x, y, z, w = offset-0):

    rec 0  w=+0.0000  xyz=(0, 0, 0)         ->   0.00 deg
    rec 1  w=-1.0000  xyz=(0, 0, +1.0000)   ->  90.00 deg
    rec 2  w=+0.0000  xyz=(0, 0, -0.7071)   -> 180.00 deg
    rec 3  w=-0.7071  xyz=(0, 0, -0.7071)   ->  90.00 deg
    rec 4  w=-0.7071  xyz=(0, 0, -0.7071)   ->  90.00 deg
    rec 5  w=-0.7071  xyz=(0, 0, 0)         ->   0.00 deg

and as [0, 90, 70.53, 70.53, 70.53, 0] under the rotation-vector reading.  A hand-built crypt
room has no 70.53-degree walls.  This is the corpus beating the in-game test, exactly as RULE ZERO
says it must: the four-way stair experiment that produced 9H.33 could not distinguish the two
readings, because the object it used was symmetric about the axis that differs.

RETRACTED: 9H.33 claimed the record holds axis * tan(theta/2) and that the engine builds
q = normalize(vector, 1.0).  The code says otherwise.  The tangent writer has been reverted; the
toolkit is back to writing the quaternion's x/y/z at 60/64/68 and the branch w in the record's
offset-0 float, and scripts/check_rotation_encoding.py now checks the code's rule against both the
round trip and the corpus.

WHAT IS STILL OPEN, LABELLED:
  * INFERRED, NOT YET READ: which FILE fields become the runtime quaternion's four slots.  The
    file's position at 48 is measured in game (our builds land in the authored grid); the file's
    offset-0 float as the quaternion's w is the toolkit's long-standing convention and is
    consistent with the corpus, but the conversion function that builds the runtime instance
    array ([world+8] + i*0x48 + 0xb0, layout {pos@0, quat@12, scale@28}) has NOT been read yet.
    That is the next decompile: find it and the layout is settled for good.
  * OPEN, and the thing the author sees: the stairs.  The crane (a 108.35 yaw, recorded as
    (0, 0, -0.8108) with w = -0.5853) renders correctly, which fits the quaternion rule.  The two
    stairs do not, and the difference is NOT in the placement rotation any more - so the next
    decompile target is the UNIT's mesh path: how a unit's mesh is fetched and framed
    (FUN_00670190's MeshObject path and the mesh objects the unit's mesh list points at), because
    the unit's mesh frame is the only other thing that can turn a correctly placed unit.

        crane       (0, 0, 1.385376)      108.35 about Z, exactly as authored
        stair #1    (-1, 0, 0)            90 about -X, exactly as authored
        stair #2    (-1, -1, 1)           120 about the diagonal - FLAT and yawed 90 in Blender
        floors x9   (0, 0, 0)             identity
    engine does with the record's 3 floats:
      (H1) q = normalize(vector, w) - the vector is a quaternion's vector part and the w slot is
           the 4th component.  This is what level_from_glb assumes.  Under it stair #1 renders
           EXACTLY -90 about X (its run exactly horizontal) and stair #2 renders its authored
           120 about the (-1,-1,1) diagonal: FLAT, but yawed 90 against stair #1.
      (H2) q = normalize(vector, 1.0) - the 3 floats are a rotation vector (axis x tan(theta/2)),
           and the w slot is ignored.  Under it stair #1 renders 70.53 about X (19.5 deg off the
           horizontal) and stair #2 renders 81.79 about (1,1,-1) - a genuine compound tilt with a
           corner dipping, which is what the author describes.
    EVIDENCE FOR H1, FROM A NATIVE LEVEL: assets/exports-samples/samples/level_24abcdf4b1fb4d4a.level
    (the skeleton, 6 records extracted from the game) holds vector lengths 0, 1.0, 0.7071 x3, 0 -
    all <= 1, and every one lands on a clean GRID yaw under H1 (90, 90, 90) and on 70.53 under H2.
    A hand-built crypt room is grid-aligned, so H1 fits the natives; and its records' w slot is
    NOT the quaternion's w (values 0.0, -1.0, 0.0, -0.7071, -0.7071, -0.7071 - several of which
    make the pair non-unit), which is why level_decode's comment says "Offset 0 is NOT w".  Our
    writer puts the w there on purpose (harmless if the engine rebuilds or ignores it).
    EVIDENCE FOR H2: the author's own eyes on stair #2 ("tilted on x and y independently, front
    right corner dipping, like with W at 1.0") and their Blender experiment (setting w = 1.0 made
    Blender agree with the game).
  * ONE OBSERVATION DECIDES IT (this was the question before the decode; 9H.34 answers it from
    the code: the record holds a normalised four-float quaternion, so the earlier H1/H2 framing is
    obsolete and 9H.33 is retracted).
  * The author's MATERIALS: the units still render with the templates' crypt-stone binding
    (0x04d8b35043c51085) because a unit's material comes from its spliced trailer, and the mesh
    block's own material name (m_floor / m_stair / m_medieval_crane) has no resource behind it
    yet.



9H.35 THE PIPELINE VERIFIED AGAINST THE GLB, WITH THE ENGINE'S OWN ARITHMETIC  *** Oct 2026 ***

scripts/check_deployed_placement.py answers, on the DEPLOYED bytes and using FUN_0042d7f0's own
arithmetic, the only question that matters: does the game's content equal the author's GLB?

  1. PLACEMENTS.  For every record of the deployed level: the quaternion the engine will build
     (from the record's four floats, normalised) against the authored node's
     R_x(+90) . R_node . R_x(-90).  Result: 0.0000 deg on all 12 placements.
  2. UNITS.  For every shipped .unit: its mesh vertices against R_x(+90) . (the GLB mesh).
     Result: 0.000000 worst vertex error on all three (9 / 166 / 19156 verts).

  Both halves pass.  So the game renders exactly the .blend for this content, and the two stairs
  are 90 degrees apart in the game because they are 90 degrees apart in the FILE
  (Stair = -90 about X; Stair.001 = 120 about the (-1,-1,1) diagonal, which is flat but yawed 90).

A NOTE ON THE TOOL ITSELF (RULE ZERO applied to my own work).  The first version of this check
reported 143.29 / 180 / 120 degrees of error on the three rotated placements - and it was the CHECK
that was wrong, not the pipeline.  The engine's matrix is written in the opposite convention from
glTF's (FUN_0042d7f0 has m01 = k(xy + wz) and m10 = k(xy - wz), the transpose of the glTF form),
so comparing a matrix from one convention against a matrix from the other is meaningless: the
transpose of a rotation is its inverse, which is exactly the 360 - 2*theta pattern those numbers
showed.  The check now compares ROTATIONS as quaternions, which is convention-free.  Same lesson
as the rest of today: verify the tool against the code before believing the number it prints.

WHAT IS STILL OPEN, NOT GUESSED:
  * The author's MATERIALS.  Our mesh blocks name m_floor / m_stair / m_medieval_crane, which do
    not exist in the material manager, so FUN_00670190's lookup fails and falls back to
    missing_shader - the magenta.  A material resource per unit is the next workstream.
  * COLLISION.  A unit's physics comes from its spliced template's trailer, so the stair and the
    crane currently collide as the crypt props they borrow from, not as their own shape - and

    9H.31's rule (the template's mesh count must equal the blocks we write) constrains the choice.



9H.36 A UNIT'S TRANSFORM IS 48 BYTES IN A 72-BYTE RECORD - THE OFFSET-0 FLOAT IS THE PREVIOUS
      UNIT'S W  *** Oct 2026 ***  -- why every rotated placement was built from the wrong w
      --- code (FUN_00430440 -> FUN_0042db10 -> FUN_0042d7f0) + the engine's own capture ---

STATUS: RESOLVED AND CONFIRMED IN GAME (2026-10-19).  The record's own offset-0 float is the
PREVIOUS unit's rotation w; the toolkit read and wrote it as its own, so every rotated placement
was built from its successor's w.  Fixed in resource_tools/level_tools.py + level_from_glb.py, and
the engine's own post-fix capture of the author's arena reports the authored quaternions exactly.

THE SYMPTOM.  "The quaternions are still messed up for duplicate models": the nine identity floors
of the author's arena are perfect and every placement that carries a rotation - the crane and the
two stairs, i.e. the repeated kinds - comes out at the wrong angle, and NONE of it is visible in
the file: every offline check passed (round trips byte-exact, ids/names/positions equal, both build
audits 0.000 deg), and the engine's own per-unit capture was dismissed in 9H.32 as "a derived
value, one foot in the next record".  It was the ground truth.

WHAT THE CODE SAYS.  FUN_00430440 (the per-unit pass) walks the instance array at

    entry_i = *(data + 8) + i*0x48 + 0xb0        and  data + 0x80 is the unit table,
                                                      so entry_i = record_i + 48

and for every unit that resolves calls

    FUN_0042db10(out, entry_i + 0xc, entry_i + 0, entry_i + 0x1c)
      -> FUN_0042d7f0(out, quaternion, position, scale)

(dumped: 0x4305b0 pushes esi+0x1c, esi, esi+0xc, out in that order; FUN_0042db10 re-pushes its own
args as arg4, arg3, arg2, out, so FUN_0042d7f0 gets (out, +0xc, +0x00, +0x1c)).  FUN_0042d7f0
reads FOUR CONSECUTIVE floats from the quaternion pointer, normalises them (k = 2/|q|^2) into a
rotation matrix, takes its third argument as the translation and its fourth as the scale.  So, per
unit, relative to the 128 + i*0x48 record the toolkit knows:

    position   record + 0x30   (48)      - the toolkit agreed
    rotation   record + 0x3c   (60)      - x, y, z AND the w at 0x48 (72)
    scale      record + 0x4c   (76)      - THREE floats here, not at record+4

and record+0x48 is, with a 72-byte stride, THE NEXT RECORD'S OFFSET-0 FLOAT.  The same function
reads id/template/material at entry-0x18/-0x10/-8 = record+0x18/0x20/0x28, so the block it views is
{id(8), template(8), material(8), pos(12), quat(16), scale(12)} = 64 bytes at record+0x18, with 8
bytes of padding to the next 72-byte boundary - a 24-byte table prologue in front and 24 bytes
after the last block (u32[3] = table_end + 24 in every shipped level, holding the last unit's
{w, scale(3), 8 padding} = 1.0,1.0,1.0,1.0,0,0,0,0).

SO THE FILE IS RIGHT AND THE TOOLKIT WAS ONE RECORD OUT.  A record's own offsets 0 and 4 are the
PREVIOUS unit's rotation w and scale.  Writing unit i's w into record i's own offset 0 - what
record_rotation_vector/_encode_entity/write_scratch all did - gives the engine:

    unit 0   w = record 1's offset 0  (unit 1's w)
    unit i   w = record i+1's offset 0
    unit N-1 w = the reserved block's first float (1.0)

which is exactly the author's report: "Stair.001's W is changed from 0.5 to 1.0 by the engine" -
the last stair took the w of the player_spawn_marker appended after the placements, and rendered
81.79 deg about the (-1,-1,1) diagonal instead of 120 ("tilted on x and y independently, front
right corner dipping").  The crane took the first stair's w (97.9 instead of 108.35, invisible on
a big prop), the first stair the second stair's (109.47 instead of 90).  Every identity placement
is immune (xyz = 0, so any w is the same rotation), which is why nine floors hid it.

MEASURED AGAINST THE ENGINE'S OWN CAPTURES (Data/runtime_units, the loader's LevelResource
sweep), joining each unit on (template hash, world position + the level's (4,4) offset):

    hub_pristine.level v modules/d01_hub_room/tutorial_room     461 joined
        w = next record's offset 0   460/461 within 1.0 deg (worst 5.402)
        w = this record's offset 0   207/461 (worst 168.293)
    room_crypt_floor_01_adventure_edited.level v crypt_floor_01  177 joined
        w = next record's offset 0   177/177 (worst 0.110)
        w = this record's offset 0   133/177 (worst 156.034)

The one hub unit (753f6db2977d8d0c, record 330) is a FILE/CAPTURE disagreement, not a rule: our
copy of that record holds xyz.x = 0.1029 where the engine's capture says 0.09205 - the same
record, a different float - so our copy is not the file that was captured.  `--max-bad 1`
documents it; every other unit is exact.

WHY THE OLD CHECKS COULD NOT SEE IT.  Every one compared the toolkit against itself:
  * decode_level -> encode_level stayed byte-exact (the field mapping is a bijection either way);
    21/21 level files still round-trip byte-exactly after the fix;
  * check_rotation_rule / check_rotation_encoding test the RULE (that the record holds a
    quaternion with a w) but not WHICH record holds it, and the corpus' own six records read as
    grid yaws under BOTH readings (0/90/180/90/90/0 or 0/180/90/90/90/0) - so 9H.34's "the corpus
    settles it" was true about the quaternion and blind to the offset;
  * the build audits compared the record against the rotation it had just been built FROM, which
    agrees with itself whatever the layout; check_deployed_placement read the record's own offset
    0 as the w - the writer's own mistake - so two wrongs agreed;
  * 9H.32's "use the capture for positions, never for rotations" threw away the only oracle: the
    capture's "one foot in the next record" was not a decoding artifact, it was the engine.

THE FIX.
  * resource_tools/level_tools.py: the transform lead is modelled - `unit_transform_lead` (unit
    i's 24 bytes are at record i+1's start; the last unit's in the reserved block),
    `table_prologue` (the 24 bytes in front of unit 0, whose first u32 is the load's byte COUNT),
    `shift_leads_forward` (moves every lead one record on - `encode_level` runs it as its last
    step, after the raw sections, so it wins over the copy of the old epilogue),
    `set_record_rotation` (see the second bug), `_parse_entity`/`decode_level` read the lead, and
    decode keeps `_lead_prologue` so a re-encode stays byte-exact.
  * level_from_glb.py: write_scratch_level shifts the leads and zeroes the PROLOGUE (not unit 0's
    flag - that would now zero unit 0's own w).  write_scratch re-reads the four floats it just
    wrote, where the engine reads them, and REFUSES the build if a placement does not reproduce
    the authored rotation.
  * check_level_capture_rotation.py (new): joins a level file to an engine capture and reports
    BOTH readings side by side, so it cannot pass by accident.
  * the raw readers updated to record+0x48 for the w: level_decode, compare_level_capture,
    compare_runtime_units, check_level_symmetry, check_rotation_encoding.

A SECOND BUG, FOUND ON THE WAY (the donor path).  `_parse_entity` keeps a record's original three
floats as `rotation_vector` so an untouched round trip is byte-exact, and `_encode_entity` prefers
that key over `rotation` - so the donor path, which set ONLY `e['rotation']` for a replaced slot,
wrote the DONOR's rotation vector with our derived w.  Every claimed placement in an arena build
was rotated by the unit it replaced.  Fixed with `level_tools.set_record_rotation`, which sets
`rotation` and drops `rotation_vector` and `flag` together.

VERIFIED AFTER THE FIX.
  * 21/21 .level files still decode -> encode byte-exactly.
  * check_deployed_placement.py (the code's own arithmetic) on the rebuilt arena: 12/12 placements
    0.0000 deg - on the --scratch build AND on a donor build (468-slot hub body).
  * the new build audit, reading the written bytes: all 12 placements reproduce the authored
    rotation (worst 0.021 deg - the export's own float32 rounding of the Stair node).
  * check_level_capture_rotation.py: the shipped reading is exact on the crypt room (177/177) and
    on 460/461 hub units; the old reading is wrong on 44/177 and 254/461.

CONFIRMED IN GAME  *** 2026-10-19, the author's own eyes and the engine's own numbers ***
The rebuilt bundle was deployed to the game (build_level_mod.py --force; see the WORKFLOW note
below) and loaded.  The loader's LevelResource sweep captured the level again, and the engine now
reports EXACTLY the authored quaternions - the same oracle that found the bug:

    unit        engine BEFORE (08:45 capture)          engine AFTER (09:13 capture)
    crane       (0, 0, -0.754, -0.657)   97.82 deg     (0, 0, -0.811, -0.585)  108.35 deg
    stair #1    (0.816, 0, 0, -0.577)   109.47 deg     (0.707, 0, 0, -0.707)    90.00 deg
    stair #2    (0.378, 0.378, -0.378, -0.756)  81.79 deg   (0.5, 0.5, -0.5, -0.5)  120.00 deg

check_level_capture_rotation.py on the deployed level and that capture: 12/12 within 1.0 deg,
WORST 0.000 deg (the old reading would still be wrong on 3, worst 30.28).  The post-fix capture is
versioned at Data/runtime_units/modules_d01_hub_room_custom_duckomp_room.json so the fix stays
provable, and the author reports the crane and both stairs standing as in Blender.

NOTE ON THE EXPORT UNDER TEST (2026-10-19).  test_arena_03.glb is not what scripts/blender_export_glb.py
produces from the same Base.blend: it carries TANGENT (that script sets export_tangents=False), no
materials, no COLOR_0, and its stair/crane meshes have 176/19159 vertices against 106/19156 (the
stair is 2 primitives in the export, 1 here).  So a different exporter setting produced it - and
that is fine: the pipeline copies the mesh verbatim and check_deployed_placement compares it
vertex-for-vertex at 0.000000.  build_level_mod.py's staleness guard refused on the 1-second
blend-vs-GLB skew; --force is the right answer when the GLB is the artifact under test.

STILL OPEN, LABELLED:
  * SCALE.  The engine reads it at record+0x4c - the next record's offset 4 - by the same
    argument, and the fix shifts it with the w.  The corpus CANNOT confirm it: no capture carries
    a scale, and the shipped levels' scales ((1,1,1), (1,7.5,1), (0,0,0), ...) are the same
    multiset under either reading.  What the fix guarantees is that a unit's scale, like its w, is
    written where the engine's pointer arithmetic reads it.  CODE-MEASURED, not corpus-measured.
  * The MATERIALS (9H.35) and COLLISION (9H.31) issues are unchanged.

9H.37 IGNORED-BY-NAME NODES, AND A MIXED-EXPORT READING THAT WAS THE TOOL'S OWN FAULT
      *** Oct 2026 ***  -- the scratch parts voted on the up axis, and a circular check said OK

WHAT CHANGED IN THE KIT.  A scene legitimately carries objects that are not part of the level:
working copies, reference geometry, scratch parts being assembled.  level_from_glb now ignores any
node whose NAME contains one of IGNORE_NAME_SUBSTRINGS (default ['throwaway'], case-insensitive;
`--ignore SUBSTR` adds more) - it is not placed, does not vote in the up-axis measurements, and
never becomes one of our units (build_own_units.py imports `ignored_node`).  It is reported as
IGNORED, separately from an unresolved unit name, because one is a decision already made and the
other is a build error.

THE TRAP THE AUTHOR'S SCENE SPRANG (and the reason the rule matters for more than tidiness).  The
10:47 export carried 34 mesh nodes, 23 of them the author's scratch parts, and the build printed

    up  : placements: 22 flat mesh(es) point +Z in the world  -> positions read as-is
    up  : meshes    : 3 flat mesh normal(s) average to +Y     -> rotations composed with -90
    *** MIXED EXPORT ... if a build looks wrong in game, this line is the first thing to re-check
    orient : 34 placement(s) NOT checked - recovered from geometry, or the export is mixed

That reading was WRONG.  The export was an ordinary Y-up one: the author's Floor.006 sits in
Blender at (8.026, -0.013, -0.185) and the file carries (8.026, -0.185, 0.013) = (bx, bz, -by),
i.e. export_yup=True was applied, and its mesh normal is +Y.  detect_world_up had counted every
flat mesh whose normal votes - including 22 panels whose NODE ROTATION turns them upright - and
those outvoted the three floors, flipping the layout half to "engine-space" and manufacturing a
mixed pair.  With the scratch parts excluded from the vote (this finding's rule), the same scene
detects +Y, the pair is the self-consistent one (deg_pos=+90, deg_mesh=-90), and the orientation
check RUNS and passes at 0.000 deg.

WHY IT IS WORTH RECORDING.  For a mixed export the tool SKIPS its node-vs-record orientation check
(that check can only express F * R_node * F^-1), so a wrong pair ships silently - the 9H.22
failure mode.  So a mixed reading means VERIFY, never "handled".

AND THE VERIFICATION I FIRST USED WAS CIRCULAR.  "Which deg_pos makes the record equal
to_engine_position(node)?" answered "0.00000" - but the record had been BUILT with that deg_pos, so
the test only confirmed what the build had already done.  A frame pair can only be confirmed
against something outside the tool:
  * the flat meshes' OWN normals (a floor tile's normal is the file's up) plus the exporter's
    setting, which for `blender_export_glb.py` is export_yup=True;
  * THE BLENDER OBJECTS THEMSELVES - the engine frame IS the Blender frame, so a record's position
    and rotation must equal the object's world transform, R_x(90) . R_node . R_x(-90) reducing to
    R_blender exactly.  Measured on the new build, all 11 placements: position 0.000000, rotation
    worst 0.0212 deg (float32 noise);
  * the engine's own runtime capture, after loading the level.

THE NEW SCENE (author's 10:57 blend, built and deployed 11:01).  24 objects: 11 real + 12
`throwaway*` + `.MIDPOINT` (an EMPTY).  All scales applied by the author (every node is 1,1,1 now,
so no entity-scale write - the crash history of 9H.34 does not apply), `Spiral Road` has geometry
now, and the names are one per shape.  Result: 23 mesh nodes -> 11 placed, 0 dropped, 12 ignored;
level 1724 B / 13 records; bundle 7.87 MB packed (17.24 MB unpacked, 54.4%): the two big units are
spiral_road (10.27 MB) and elevator_chains (5.57 MB), both far above the old crane's 2.3 MB.  All
nine units wear the floor template as a PLACEHOLDER (physics + material come from the trailer) -
replace per kind when the real template is chosen.  Extent 65 x 75 x 62 units, which is much larger
than the hub room module it ships as.

9H.38 PHYSICS BY NAME: A UNIT'S COLLIDER IS ITS TEMPLATE'S TRAILER, SO A ROLE PICKS A TEMPLATE
      *** Oct 2026 ***  -- the request, what the builder can and cannot do, and the trailer map

THE REQUEST.  "I was unable to walk on any of my custom mesh.  Are we able to add builder flags
for that - if a name contains _walkable or _collision then the builder makes them that way, and
without them they are treated without collision?"

WHAT THE BUILDER CANNOT DO.  It cannot invent a collider.  A unit's physics IS its template's
trailer, carried over verbatim by `splice_into_template` (9H.19.2: the engine creates meshes and
actors from the compiled unit; 9H.20 measured a spliced plane's collider in game as the DONOR's
8x8 patch; 9H.31: the collider is the template's, so a stair collides as the crypt stair it
borrowed).  The physics blob's own layout is still undecoded - 9H.19.3 flagged it as the next
decode - so there is no way to synthesise a collider from an arbitrary mesh today.  What CAN be
done, and is now built, is choosing WHICH physics a mesh wears.

THE TRAILER, MEASURED ACROSS 575 EXTRACTED UNITS.  A unit is [header][mesh][materials][trailer]
[footer].  The trailer begins with 16 bytes {0, PHYSICS_SIZE, 0, count} - the SECOND WORD is the
size of the physics blob that follows it, and 0 means no physics:

    cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c    80 B   nav 'floor'      a walkable patch (9H.20 mapped it)
    crypt_wall_2c             136 B   nav 'blocker'    solid
    crypt_pillar_01           164 B   nav 'blocker'    solid
    crypt_stairs_low_4c       220 B   nav 'slope'      walkable slope
    decals/decal, fog_volume   80 B   nav 'ignore'     (a name rule forces 'ignore')
    lights/light, skydome,
      core/rendering/light_proxies  0 B   no nav string   NO PHYSICS - walk-through

After the blob comes a data table whose records are {key_thin_hash, ?, type, size, value-inline}
(`nav_grid_type_auto`, type 3, 8 bytes padded - 'floor', 'blocker', 'slope', 'ignore'), and the
last 32 bytes are the footer, which names a MATERIAL SWATCH: the floor's and the pillar's are both
`blocks/d01_crypt/d01_crypt_swatch`, while every zero-physics unit's footer names ITSELF
(`lights/light`, the skydome) instead - so wearing one of those loses the material binding, and
`lights/light` also carries 'FlowCallbacks'/'light' (it would light the level).  That is why the
`none` role does NOT use them.

THE ROLES, AS BUILT.  `build_own_units.py --physics-template=ROLE=PATH` (repeatable) sets a
template per role; `--physics BASE=ROLE` sets a per-kind fallback; and a node NAME overrides both
with `_walkable` / `_collision` (the resolution is name > config > 'none').  The build prints the
physics it actually got, READ BACK OUT OF THE BUILT FILE:

    walkable   -> cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c      80 B, nav 'floor'
    collision  -> crypt_pillar_01             164 B, nav 'blocker'
    none       -> strip:cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c   0 B, nav 'ignore'

`strip:PATH` is a template spec: the template with its physics blob REMOVED (header size set to 0,
the `size` bytes dropped) and its nav type rewritten to 'ignore' - which is what the game's own
builder writes for a unit that collides with nothing (9H.19).  It keeps the swatch material and
the rest of the trailer, which the shipped zero-physics units cannot.  OFFLINE it is consistent:
the floor trailer 1437 B -> 1357 B, physics 80 B -> 0 B, nav 'floor' -> 'ignore', the 32-byte
swatch footer preserved, and the built unit reads back as 0 B / 'ignore'.  THE ENGINE'S OWN PARSE
OF A STRIPPED TRAILER IS NOT CONFIRMED HERE - it is the thing to watch in the next game run (a
unit with `none` should simply be walked through).  If it is not, the fallback is a shipped
zero-physics template at the cost of its material.

DEPLOYED 2026-10-19 ~11:40.  The 9 kinds: `Floor` -> walkable (the config's per-kind fallback),
the other 8 -> none (no flag, no override).  Level 1724 B / 13 records, 11 placements, orientation
check 0.000 deg, bundle 7.95 MB packed.  `check_deployed_bundle.py` reports "template not in
.unit orig - count unchecked" for the stripped units, which is honest: the mesh count was
enforced against the stripped template (1 mesh) at splice time, and the verifier simply cannot
look a synthesised trailer up by name.

WHAT IS STILL OPEN, AND THE ONE THING TO MEASURE NEXT.
  * WALKING.  The floors already carry a walkable 8x8 patch, and 9H.20 mapped exactly that patch
    with the ActorProbe raycast grid, so "cannot walk on my mesh" is NOT explained by the missing
    flags.  Two candidate causes, neither testable offline: (a) the collider is the TEMPLATE's 8x8
    patch anchored at the unit's origin, so a mesh of another SIZE or SHAPE is never walkable as
    itself - the game's own answer is to tile a surface with 8x8 floor blocks, which the author's
    three Floor tiles already are; (b) something about the --scratch level body.  The next run
    should use ActorProbe (mods/functions/ActorProbe, the 5x5 floor-raycast grid of 9H.20) on a
    floor placement, and say whether the player fell through everything or stood but could not
    move.
  * The mesh-size question the author raised ("something too big covering the screen") is a property
    of the models, not the pipeline: measured world AABBs of the deployed level - elevator_chains
    6.3 x 6.3 x 75.2, elevator_shaft 9.6 x 9.6 x 69.7, spiral_road (and _follow) 56.3 x 56.3 x 44.9,
    start_scaffolding 4.8 x 6.3 x 24.4 (its top 1.6 above the floor, ~5 units from the spawn),
    floors 8 x 8 x 0.0.

9H.38.1 THE FIRST `none` BUILD CRASHED AT LEVEL LOAD (2026-10-19 11:23) - AND IT IS NOT YET
       ATTRIBUTED  *** the strip and the light template are BOTH unproven in game ***

THE CRASH, FROM crash_trace.log.  Three dumps, of which the first is the known-benign AlienFX one
(0xE06D7363 raised through LightFX.dll/kernelbase) and the other two are identical:

    exception 0xC0000005 at 00402e4c   RVA 0x00002E4C
    access    write at 00000004         RVA 0x00000004

0x402e4c is +0x3C into FUN_00402e10, a container `resize(new_size)`:

    402e14  mov  ecx,[esi+4]        ; capacity (this = esi)
    402e1b  cmp  edi,ecx / jbe ...  ; grow via FUN_00402e70 if the new size exceeds it
    402e40  mov  ecx,[esi]          ; size
    402e42  mov  eax,[esi+8]        ; DATA POINTER
    402e45  lea  eax,[eax+ecx*4]    ; &data[size]
    402e4c  mov  DWORD PTR [eax],0  ; <-- the fault: write to 0x4

The container is {size@+0, capacity@+4, data@+8}; eax = data + size*4 = 4, so DATA WAS NULL WHILE
SIZE WAS ALREADY 1 - an inconsistent (freed, or sized without a buffer) container that the level
load then zero-fills.  FUN_00402e10's callers include FUN_0042b6b0, the level loader, which resizes
containers as it builds the Level (its decompile calls it with `*(level_data + 4)` - the unit count -
right beside a run of `param_1[0x26..0x3f]` container fields).  The stack scan puts FUN_00424470 (a
0x340-byte instantiation helper that walks a {size@0x40, data@0x48} container) on the frame.

WHEN.  modloader.log: the package `resource_packages/custom/custom_run` resolved at 11:23:11, the
menu UI loaded, the Custom Run button was armed at 11:23:15 - and the log stops there.  level_mining
captured the TITLESCREEN (45 units) and never reached our level, so the fault is inside our level's
load, not the package registration.

WHAT IS AND IS NOT KNOWN.  The level file is unchanged since the build that loaded and rendered
(11:01); the ONLY change in the 11:17 build was the eight `none` units' trailers.  But BOTH
candidate trailers are untested in game - `strip:` (590 B, physics 0, nav ignore, swatch kept) AND
the shipped `lights/light` (1037 B, physics 0, light flow, no swatch) - so the crash cannot yet be
attributed to either, and the earlier "the light template works" assumption was never tested.  What
is measured: a trailer the ENGINE ITSELF shipped (the floor's, 670 B / 80 B / 'floor') loads, and
the strip's own shape is not obviously wrong (its 16-byte header {0,0,0,2} matches the skydome's).
The strip remains REJECTED until the loader's parse is read: it removes 80 bytes from the middle of
the trailer, and if anything in the trailer or the unit header holds a trailer-relative offset, that
move breaks it silently.

WHAT WAS DONE.  The build was returned to BISECT STEP A: all three roles point at the floor
template, i.e. exactly the physics of the build that loaded (every unit walkable-patched, 670 B
trailer, verifier: "mesh count 1 = template declares 1").  Deployed 11:26.  The roles themselves stay
in the config (name flags, per-kind map, per-role templates), so the bisect is one line per run:

    A  all roles -> the floor template            PROVEN (this build)
    B  none -> lights/light                       0 B physics, is a zero-physics trailer safe at all?
    C  none -> strip:<floor>                      only if B is safe, and only after the loader is read

THE AUTHOR'S "road follow" SUSPICION, CHECKED.  `Spiral Road` and `Spiral Road Follow` are NOT the
same mesh and never were: road = 157,180 verts in the built unit (62,368 in the .blend), follow =
195 verts, with the SAME local bbox (56.3 x 56.3 x ~45) - i.e. the follow is a low-poly shell of the
road, which matches "meant to be nearly the same".  Their trailers were identical in the 11:17 build
(both `none`), so nothing singles the follow out.

9H.39 SIXTEEN-BIT INDICES, ALWAYS: 65,535 VERTS PER MESH BLOCK  *** Oct 2026 ***
      --- the corpus rule, the two meshes that broke it, and why the road warped halfway ---

THE SYMPTOM.  "Spiral Road is getting messed up about halfway down, and its geometry looks like it
is being pulled upwards."

WHAT THE ENGINE'S OWN DATA DOES.  Across 582 shipped units / 1,242 mesh blocks:

    bytes per index        2 (uint16) for ALL 1,242 blocks - no shipped mesh uses 32-bit indices
    largest shipped mesh   15,972 verts (0x27e2679c6a12fc0c.unit)
    mesh blocks per unit   439 units have 1; 67 have 2; 12 have 3; 7 have 4; 9 have 5; 5 have 6;
                           10 have 9; 5 have 11

So the de-facto limit is 65,535 verts PER MESH BLOCK, with more geometry carried by ADDING BLOCKS -
which is what the game's own units do (the crypt stairs wear 5 blocks).

WHY THE ROAD WARPED.  Our encoder writes 32-bit indices when a mesh needs them
(`gltf_to_unit._build_mesh_block`, `max_idx > 65535`), a branch the corpus never exercises, and the
only two meshes this toolkit has ever written that way are:

    Spiral Road      157,180 exported verts (62,368 in Blender - the exporter splits at UV seams)
    Elevator Chains   74,536 exported verts (58,080 in Blender)

and those are exactly the two that render corrupted.  For the road the corruption starts at
65,536/157,180 = 42% in - "about halfway down" - which is where a reader taking 2-byte indices from
our 4-byte buffer would have consumed the real data and started indexing garbage; triangles then
stretch to whatever vertex the garbage names, which is what "pulled upwards" looks like.
MEASURED IN CORPUS + MATCHED TO THE SYMPTOM; the engine's own index read has not been decompiled, so
"32-bit is illegal" is still an inference - but splitting into 16-bit blocks is what the game does
regardless, so it is the safe fix either way.

CAN A BIG MESH BE WELDED DOWN INSTEAD?  No.  Distinct (position, normal, UV) triples:
Spiral Road 157,180 of 157,180 (every vertex is unique - the exporter's splits are real UV/normal
seams), Elevator Chains 74,536 of 74,536.  Welding would change the shading, so splitting is the
only route.

THE FIX IN THE TOOLKIT (2026-10-19).
  * `build_own_units.py` REFUSES a node whose mesh exports more than MAX_VERTS_PER_MESH (65,535),
    with the count, the corpus rule and the remedy in the message; it also flags any unit over
    the game's own largest mesh (15,972) and says what fraction of the limit it is.
  * `gltf_to_unit._build_mesh_block` still writes 32-bit indices if asked (other callers exist), but
    now says so LOUDLY on stderr, quoting the corpus numbers.
  * The author's route - split the object in Blender so each piece is its own unit, exactly as the
    game's own content is built - is the intended one, and it needs no template juggling: a small
    mesh is a single-block unit and wears whatever role template is configured (a 1-mesh template
    cannot host a big mesh at all, because a body MUST declare as many mesh blocks as its template
    - 9H.31 - so splitting into 5 blocks would force a 5-mesh template and drag its physics along).

NOTE FOR THE NEXT BUILD: the level deployed at 11:26 still contains the broken road and chains
(both written before this guard existed).  The guard now stops a rebuild until the two objects are
split, so the split is a prerequisite for the next deploy.

9H.40 THE AUTHOR SPLIT THE TWO BIG MESHES, AND A NAMING BUG FELL OUT  *** Oct 2026 ***

The route taken was the one 9H.39 recommended - split in Blender, each piece its own unit:

    Elevator Chains   968 objects, 60 verts each (77 exported)  -> ONE unit placed 968 times
    Spiral Road       7 objects, 3,101-5,399 verts (9,506-16,608 exported) -> 7 units, 7 placements

So every shipped mesh is now under 16,608 verts - the same order as the game's own largest (15,972)
- and all 15 units use 16-bit indices (checked on the deployed bytes: 15 of 15 blocks, 2 bytes each).

MEASURED RESULT (deployed 2026-10-19 11:41).
    996 mesh nodes -> 984 placed, 0 dropped, 12 ignored (the throwaway markers)
    level 986 records / 83,456 B   (968 chain links + 3 floors + 7 road pieces + 6 others + ours)
    rotation + orientation checks: 984 placements, worst 0.000 deg
    check_deployed_placement.py: 984 of 984 at 0.0000 deg
    bundle: 4.39 MB packed / 7.32 MB unpacked   (was 7.95 / 17.24 MB)
  The chain unit alone went from 5,568,816 B (one 74,536-vert mesh, 32-bit indices) to 5,912 B (a
  77-vert link, 16-bit) - 940x smaller - because 968 instances now reuse one tiny unit instead of one
  giant mesh being drawn as a chain.

THE NAMING BUG THIS EXPOSED (fixed).  `build_own_units.base_name` stripped a trailing dot-suffix
whenever it STARTED with a digit, while `level_from_glb.base_name` required it to be ALL digits.  The
author's road pieces are named `Spiral Road.002safdasdf`, `.003745sdgd`, `.004fdghdfghf`,
`.005jfgjr`, `.006fdhdfhdfgh` - five DIFFERENT shapes whose suffixes start with a digit - so the unit
builder collapsed all five into one `Spiral Road` base and built it from the first piece's mesh,
while the level converter (all-digits rule) saw five separate names.  The two MUST agree or the level
references units the builder never built: they now both strip only Blender's own purely-numeric
suffix (`Floor.001`, `Floor.003#7` -> `Floor`), verified on eight name shapes.  `Spiral RoadFirst`
and `Spiral RoadLast` were always distinct (no dot) - and note the tool would have kept any name
that has no digits, so a tidier naming scheme needs no code at all.

HOUSEKEEPING: three unit files the config no longer references (the old crane 2.3 MB, the old
157,180-vert spiral_road 10.3 MB and the old stair) were removed from arena_mod/units/.  They were
never shipped - the bundle builder packages the config's `resources` list (measured: 17 resources =
package + level + 15 units, no stale entries).

STILL OPEN: the `none`/physics bisect (B: lights/light, C: strip:) - every role is still on the
proven floor template (step A) - and the walking question, which needs the ActorProbe grid on a floor
tile in game.

9H.40.1 CONFIRMED IN GAME (2026-10-19 11:44) - and the join a level of duplicates needs

The author loaded the 984-placement arena and reports it RENDERS CORRECTLY: the spiral road is whole
again (the 16-bit split fixed the warping) and the 968-link chain is right.

THE ENGINE'S OWN NUMBERS.  crash_trace.log for that run holds ONE dump - the known-benign AlienFX
0xE06D7363 first-chance - and NO 0xC0000005 at all, so the 9H.38.1 crash did not recur.
level_mining captured the level at 11:44:03 with 984 units, and runtime_units.tsv shows exactly what
the file says: 968 x #ID[07e203c799450ae5] (the chain link), 3 x #ID[6892a66dda35e093] (the floors),
and one each of the other 13 kinds - no missing unit, no extra one.

HOW TO CHECK A LEVEL WHOSE UNITS SIT ON TOP OF EACH OTHER.  The checked-in capture is
Data/runtime_units/modules_d01_hub_room_custom_duckomp_room.json (984 units, superseding the 12-unit
one from the earlier build), and `check_level_capture_rotation.py` grows a `--by-index` mode for it:
the 968 links stand about 0.08 apart, so every (template hash, position) key collides and the
default join can only match the 16 ISOLATED units (measured: 16 of 986).  `--by-index` lines capture
unit i up with file record i - valid, because the engine reports a level's units in the file's order
- and proves the alignment first by measuring every position:

    --by-index : 984/984 within 1.0 deg, worst 0.000 deg;  worst position error 0.00005
                 (offset (4,4,0) detected automatically; the OLD reading fails on 982/984, worst
                 90 deg - the two rotated placements - so this level DOES distinguish them)
    default    :  16/986 joined, 16/16 exact

The capture also settles the whole pipeline at scale: 984 authored placements, 15 units, every mesh
under 16,608 verts and every index 16-bit, all of it reproducing exactly what the .blend holds.

9H.41 THE COLLIDER SITS ON THE *TEMPLATE'S* MESH BOX - AND OUR MESHES WERE CENTRED, WHICH IS WHY THE PLAYER FALLS THROUGH

SYMPTOM.  In the 984-placement arena (build confirmed in game, 9H.40.1) the player falls through
pretty much everything.

CAUSE, measured offline in one command: the template's geometry and ours are anchored differently.
A splice's collider is the TEMPLATE's - so where is it?  9H.20 answered that in game with
ActorProbe: the splash plane spliced into cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c came out with an 8x8 collider
patch spanning x,y in [0,+8) anchored at the unit's own origin.  That is EXACTLY the template's own
mesh box:

    template cryptsmooth_floor_4c_4c  v=9  min=(0.000, 0.000, 0.000)  max=(8.000, 8.000, 0.000)
    our floor unit (as deployed)      v=9  min=(-4.000, -4.000, 0.000) max=(4.000, 4.000, 0.000)

The author's tile is centred on its origin; the template's is anchored at its corner.  So the
collider patch sits FOUR UNITS AWAY in x and y from the visible tile: three quarters of every floor
tile has nothing under it, and the spawn marker - which the build places at the median tile's
centre, i.e. exactly the patch's CORNER - drops the player straight through.

THE FIX, in the pipeline rather than by re-anchoring 984 objects in Blender.  A unit's collider is
anchored in the UNIT'S OWN frame - it is compiled into the template and follows the placement - so
the only thing that can be moved onto it is the MESH:

  * build_own_units.py moves the generated mesh by `template_box_min - mesh_box_min` (x and y only,
    after the frame rotation) and writes the vector to --offsets (unit_offsets.json, keyed both by
    unit path and by node base name);
  * level_from_glb.py reads it with --unit-offsets and subtracts it from every placement of that
    unit THROUGH THE RECORD'S OWN ROTATION AND SCALE - pos -= R(q) . (s * d) - because that is what
    the engine applies to the mesh before translating it;
  * build_level_mod.py deletes the file up front and threads both flags, so one run is
    self-consistent, and every build_own_units run merges in only the kinds it built;
  * check_deployed_placement.py takes the same file and measures both halves of the truth: the
    record plus what the engine applies has to land on the authored node, and the unit's mesh minus
    the vector has to equal the GLB's mesh.

Net effect: nothing visible moves - the level still renders what the .blend shows - and the
collider now covers the mesh.  For a floor tile that is exact, because the shifted unit's box comes
out (0,0,0)->(8,8,0), identical to the template's, so the 8x8 patch covers the whole visible tile.

WHY X AND Y ONLY.  The collider of every template this toolkit uses is a HORIZONTAL patch (the
floor's is 8x8 at z = 0), so x,y decide whether there is anything under the player, while z is the
height that patch sits at - which the author already controls with the object's own z (in the arena
the record's z IS the floor surface, so the patch is at the surface).  Aligning z as well would sink
a walkable patch to the underside of a thick slab.

WHAT THIS DOES NOT DO.  It does not give a big object a big collider.  The patch is still the
TEMPLATE's 8x8, so a 56-unit road piece has an 8x8 patch at its own box corner and each of the 968
chain links gets one too.  Real per-mesh collision means synthesising actors into the trailer's
physics blob, which requires that blob decoded; the actor types the engine knows are named in its
own strings (CollisionTriangles / CollisionPlanes / CollisionConvexMasks / CollisionSpheres), so the
parse is findable (9H.19).

TO CONFIRM IN GAME.  Walk onto a floor tile: the patch is now under the visible tile instead of
diagonally off it.  ActorProbe on a tile should report the 8x8 footprint AT the tile.

9H.41.1 THE CHAIN'S ORIGINS: 968 UNITS IN ONE SPOT BECOME 968 ALONG THE CHAIN

The author's 968 chain links exported with 968 IDENTICAL node translations - one distinct position,
measured in the GLB - while the link meshes carried the real offsets, which is why the chain still
rendered right in game (9H.40.1).  It also meant every link's unit origin, and therefore every
collider patch, was in the same place: the chain had collision in one spot and none along its
length.  Re-applying each object's origin fixed the export: 968 nodes, 968 distinct translations,
about 0.31 apart and running z = +41.93 down to -32.82 and back (a 74.7-unit loop).

The rebuild that followed needed NO change to the toolkit, and that is the point of 9H.41's design:
the collider vector is re-derived per build from the mesh that is actually there
(`template_box_min - mesh_box_min`), so moving 968 origins is just another input.  The chains' vector
is (0.03, 0.13, 0) because their origins now sit by their geometry; every link's placement moved
back by exactly that, and the level still renders what Blender shows:

    worst placement error 0.0000 deg over 984 placements (968 of them chain links), every unit's
    mesh exact to 0.000002, bundle verified, deployed 12:02

The rotations the author lost while separating the links are still to redo - and they are safe to
restore per link: the compensation goes through EACH PLACEMENT'S OWN ROTATION (pos -= R(q) . (s*d)),
so a rotated link's patch rotates with it and its mesh still lands on the patch.

9H.42 APPLY MODIFIERS AT EXPORT TIME, NEVER IN THE .BLEND - OR THE ROAD IS SILENTLY MISSING

The author's big-update scene has 1485 MESH objects, 13 CURVE objects and 1 EMPTY.  The 13 curves are
the `Road Top` sections: each carries a GEOMETRY NODES modifier (`Mult Curve Instancing`) that
generates the actual road.  blender_export_glb.py asked the glTF exporter for `export_apply: False`,
so those modifiers were never evaluated - and the exporter wrote the 13 `Road Top` nodes WITH NO
MESH.  Blender showed the road; the GLB did not; the unit builder built no `road_top`; the level
placed 1486 of 1499 nodes and the bundle then failed on the missing resource.  A node without a mesh
cannot become a unit or a placement, so the whole thing surfaced as one error far from its cause.

THE FIX.  `export_apply: True` - the exporter evaluates modifiers for the export and the .blend
keeps them live, which is exactly what the author asked for ("apply them during export to keep them
in the blend file but have the translations in game").  Measured before trusting it, because a
BAKED export - transforms applied into the meshes, no node transforms at all - is what
9H.22.3/9H.23.1 had to build a recovery path for:

    nodes 1499, with a mesh 1498 (was 1485), with translation 1499, with rotation 690
    Big Spike  node t=(9.653, -26.138, -30.680)  ==  Blender loc (9.6531, 30.6801, -26.1382) in Y-up

Every node keeps its own transform, so this is NOT a baked export: the level pipeline is unaffected.
`--no-apply` restores the old behaviour for an A/B on one file.

AND IT CANNOT HAPPEN QUIETLY AGAIN.  The export now ends with a warning listing every object that
arrived with no mesh but COULD have one (a mesh/curve/surface/meta/font object - an empty is
legitimate and skipped):

    WARNING: N object(s) that should have geometry arrived with NO mesh: ...

THE TEST BUILD (deployed 14:53).  The config went from 15 to 17 own kinds: 11 added for the new
objects (Big Spike, Elevator Scaffold Top, FLOOR BOTTOM, PLAYER_SPAWN, Road Top, TODO_*, Topside
Floor, Topside Floor Half, Waterfall Back) and 9 dropped because their objects no longer exist in the
scene (the old spiral road pieces and Start Scaffolding).  The author's own PLAYER_SPAWN object now
drives the spawn: build_level_mod.py grew `--spawn-at`, forwarded to level_from_glb.py, so the level
no longer guesses the median floor tile - here (0.03, -0.01, 14.25).  Every node accounted for:

    GLB mesh nodes 1486 == level placements 1486, per kind exact (1280 chains, 65 topside floors,
    64 floors, 50 shafts, 13 road sections, ...);  worst placement error 0.0000 deg; every unit's
    mesh exact (worst 0.000004, Road Top's 18107 verts included); bundle verified and deployed.

WHAT IS STILL OFF, ON PURPOSE (the author asked for a test build): `Road Top` is 13 DIFFERENT
shapes under one base name, and one base name is one unit - all 13 placements render the first
section.  `Floor` similarly mixes 8x8 tiles with 8x4 halves.  Distinct shapes need distinct base
names (`Road Top A`, `Road Top B`, ...) - a purely numeric tail like `.001` is stripped as Blender's
duplicate suffix (9H.40), so letters or words are what works.
